├── mathematical-induction/
├── statement/
Theorem: Mathematical Induction

Let \(P(n)\) be a statement depending on \(n \in \mathbb{N}\).

Assume:

  1. Base case: \(P(1)\) is true.
  2. Inductive step: For every \(m\in\mathbb{N}\), if \(P(m)\) is true, then \(P(m+1)\) is true.

Then

\[ P(n) \text{ is true for every } n \in \mathbb{N}. \]

Remark. In the inductive step, \(P(m)\) is called the induction hypothesis.

Example application

Let

\[ P(n):\quad 2^n\geq n+1,\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}. \]

Base case. Verify \(P(1)\) is true. Indeed \(2^1=2\geq 1+1\).

Inductive step. Verify \(P(m)\Rightarrow P(m+1)\).

We thus assume the induction hypothesis \(P(m)\) is true, i.e., \(2^m\geq m+1\), and prove \(P(m+1)\).

Observe \(P(m)\) being true implies

\[ 2^{m+1}=2\cdot 2^m\geq 2(m+1)\geq m+2. \]

Thus \(P(m+1)\) is true.

Conclusion: \(2^n\geq n+1\) for \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), by mathematical induction.

Remark. To prove mathematical induction, we appeal to the well-ordering property of \(\mathbb{N}\) and proof by contradiction.

├── well-ordering-property/

Recall:

\[ \mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}. \]

\(\mathbb{N}\) is ordered:

\[ 1<2<3<\cdots. \]

Well-ordering property. If \(S\subset\mathbb{N}\) is nonempty, then \(S\) has a least element.

That is, there exists \(x\in S\) such that

\[ x\leq y \qquad \text{for every } y\in S. \]

We take the well-ordering property as an axiom: a basic property of \(\mathbb{N}\) that we assume without proof.

├── proof-by-contradiction/

To prove a statement by contradiction:

  1. Assume the statement is false.
  2. Derive a contradiction.
  3. Conclude that the original statement is true.

Example

Theorem. \(\sqrt{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}\).

Proof. Suppose, for contradiction, that \(\sqrt{2}=\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}\) in lowest terms.

Then \(p^2=2q^2\), so \(p^2\) is even, hence \(p\) is even.

Why? If \(k^2\) is even, then \(k\) is even. There are two standard ways to see this:

  1. Euclid's lemma. Since \(2\mid k^2=k\cdot k\) and \(2\) is prime, Euclid's lemma gives \(2\mid k\). Hence \(k\) is even.
  2. Elementary parity argument. If \(k\) were odd, then \(k=2m+1\) for some integer \(m\), and \[ k^2=(2m+1)^2=2(2m^2+2m)+1, \] which is odd, a contradiction.

Either argument proves that \(k^2\) even implies \(k\) even. The second simply uses a short contradiction inside the larger proof by contradiction.

Write \(p=2k\).

Then \(q^2=2k^2\), so \(q^2\) is even, hence \(q\) is even.

Thus \(p\) and \(q\) are both even and therefore divisible by \(2\).

This contradicts that \(\frac{p}{q}\) was in lowest terms.

Therefore \(\sqrt{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}\). \(\square\)

├── proof-of-induction/

Let

\[ S:=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid P(n)\text{ is false}\}. \]

We want to show

\[ S=\emptyset. \]

Suppose, for contradiction, that

\[ S\neq\emptyset. \]

Proof idea: use the well-ordering property of \(\mathbb{N}\) to show that the smallest member of \(S\) both belongs and does not belong to \(S\), thereby furnishing a contradiction.

By the well-ordering property of \(\mathbb{N}\), \(S=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid P(n)\text{ is false}\}\) has a least element \(m\).

By the assumption \(P(1)\) is true (i.e., the base case),

\[ m\neq 1, \]

so

\[ m>1. \]

In particular \(m-1\in\mathbb N\).

Since \(m\) is the least element of \(S\), \(m-1\notin S\), so again \(m-1\leq nx\).

\[ m-1\notin S. \]

Since

\[ S=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid P(n)\text{ is false}\} \]

and \(m-1\notin S\), it follows that

\[ P(m-1)\text{ is true}. \]

By the assumption that \(P(n)\Rightarrow P(n+1)\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) (i.e., the induction step), taking \(n=m-1\),

\[ P(m)\text{ is true}. \]

Therefore

\[ m\notin S,\qquad S=\{n\in\mathbb N\mid P(n)\text{ is false}\}. \]

But \(m\in S\). Contradiction.

Thus

\[ S=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid P(n)\text{ is false}\}=\emptyset. \]

Therefore

\[ P(n)\text{ is true for every }n\in\mathbb{N}. \]
├── example-1/
Example 1: Geometric sum

Theorem. For \(c\neq 1\) and \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ 1+c+c^2+\cdots+c^n=\frac{1-c^{n+1}}{1-c}. \]

Proof. We use induction on \(n\).

To begin: What is the base case?

Base case: \(n=1\).

If \(n=1\), then

\[ 1+c+\cdots+c^n=1+c, \]

and

\[ \frac{1-c^{n+1}}{1-c} =\frac{1-c^2}{1-c} =\frac{(1-c)(1+c)}{1-c} =1+c. \]

Thus the base case holds:

\[ 1+c+\cdots+c^n=\frac{1-c^{n+1}}{1-c}\qquad \text{when } n=1. \]

Induction hypothesis: Fix an arbitrary \(m\in\mathbb{N}\) and assume the identity holds for \(m\):

\[ 1+c+c^2+\cdots+c^m=\frac{1-c^{m+1}}{1-c}. \]

Need to show the induction hypothesis implies

\[ 1+c+c^2+\cdots+c^m+c^{m+1}=\frac{1-c^{m+2}}{1-c}. \]

Inductive step:

Using the induction hypothesis, we obtain

\[ \begin{aligned} 1+c+c^2+\cdots+c^m+c^{m+1} &=\textcolor{red}{(1+c+c^2+\cdots+c^m)}+c^{m+1}\\ &=\textcolor{red}{\frac{1-c^{m+1}}{1-c}}+c^{m+1}\\ &=\frac{1-c^{m+1}+c^{m+1}(1-c)}{1-c}\\ &=\frac{1-c^{m+2}}{1-c}. \end{aligned} \]

Thus the identity holds for \(m+1\).

Having proved the base case and inductive step, mathematical induction gives

\[ 1+c+c^2+\cdots+c^n=\frac{1-c^{n+1}}{1-c} \]

for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).

└── example-2/
Example 2

Theorem. If \(c\geq -1\), then

\[ (1+c)^n\geq 1+nc \]

for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).

Proof. We use induction on \(n\).

To begin: What is the base case?

Base case: \(n=1\).

If \(n=1\), then

\[ (1+c)^n=1+c, \]

and

\[ 1+nc=1+c. \]

Thus the base case holds:

\[ (1+c)^n\geq 1+nc\qquad \text{when } n=1. \]

Induction hypothesis: Fix an arbitrary \(m\in\mathbb{N}\) and assume the inequality holds for \(m\):

\[ (1+c)^m\geq 1+mc. \]

Need to show the induction hypothesis implies

\[ (1+c)^{m+1}\geq 1+(m+1)c. \]

Inductive step:

Since \(c\geq -1\), we have \(1+c\geq 0\). Using the induction hypothesis, we obtain

\[ \begin{aligned} (1+c)^{m+1} &=(1+c)^m(1+c)\\ &\geq(1+mc)(1+c)\\ &=1+(m+1)c+mc^2\\ &\geq 1+(m+1)c. \end{aligned} \]

Thus the inequality holds for \(m+1\).

Having proved the base case and inductive step, mathematical induction gives

\[ (1+c)^n\geq 1+nc \]

for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).

├── cardinality-and-countability/
├── fundamental-question/
Fundamental Question

Which sets have “more” elements?

\[ \{1,2,3\},\qquad \mathbb N,\qquad \{\text{even integers}\},\qquad \mathbb Q,\qquad \mathbb R,\qquad \mathbb R^3? \]

Which have the same number of elements?

There are many ways to measure the “size” of a set. The most fundamental for our purposes is cardinality.

Cardinality formalizes the idea of comparing the number of elements in two sets.

This section develops this notion of size.

├── functions/

Let \(A\) and \(B\) be sets.

A function

\[ f:A\to B \]

assigns each \(x\in A\) to a unique element of \(B\), denoted \(f(x)\).

We call \(A\) and \(B\) the domain and codomain of \(f\), respectively.

We say that \(x\) is mapped to \(f(x)\).

Remark. In this formalization, we are taking care to view a function as having a specified domain and codomain. In particular, the two functions

\[ \begin{aligned} f:[0,1]&\to\mathbb{R}, & f(x)&=x^2,\\ g:\mathbb{R}&\to\mathbb{R}, & g(x)&=x^2 \end{aligned} \]

are not the same function.

Image

Let \(C\subset A\). The image of \(C\) under \(f:A\to B\) is

\[ f(C):=\{y\in B:y=f(x)\text{ for some }x\in C\}. \]

So \(f(C)\) is the set of outputs obtained from elements of \(C\).

Preimage

Let \(D\subset B\). The preimage of \(D\) under \(f:A\to B\) is

\[ f^{-1}(D):=\{x\in A:f(x)\in D\}. \]

So \(f^{-1}(D)\) is the set of inputs that are mapped into \(D\).

Example

Let

\[ A=\{-2,-1,0,1,2\},\qquad B=\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}. \]

Define \(f:A\to B\) by \(f(x)=x^2\).

Then

\[ f(A)=\{0,1,4\},\qquad f^{-1}(\{2,3\})=\emptyset,\qquad f^{-1}(\{4\})=\{-2,2\}. \]
├── injective-surjective-bijective-functions/

Let \(A\) and \(B\) be sets and let

\[ f:A\to B \]

be a function.

There are three fundamental types of functions: injections, surjections and bijections.

Injections

\(f\) is injective or one-to-one (1-1) if

\[ f(x_1)=f(x_2)\implies x_1=x_2. \]
Surjections

\(f\) is surjective or onto if

\[ f(A)=B. \]
Bijections

\(f\) is bijective if it is both injective and surjective.

Inverse functions

If \(f:A\to B\) is bijective, then there is an inverse function

\[ f^{-1}:B\to A \]

which assigns each \(y\in B\) to the unique \(x\in A\) such that

\[ f(x)=y. \]

Thus,

\[ f\bigl(f^{-1}(y)\bigr)=y. \]
Notational Remark

Common notation:

\[ \begin{aligned} f:A\hookrightarrow B &\qquad \text{injection},\\ f:A\twoheadrightarrow B &\qquad \text{surjection},\\ f:A\xrightarrow{\sim} B &\qquad \text{bijection}. \end{aligned} \]

Warning. In these notes, \(f^{-1}\) denotes the inverse function when it exists. For a set \(D\subset B\), \(f^{-1}(D)\) denotes the preimage of \(D\); if \(f\) is bijective, this is equivalently the image of \(D\) under the inverse map \(f^{-1}:B\to A\).

├── cardinality/
Question

When do two sets have the same size?

Cantor's idea: two sets have the same size when we can pair each element of one set with a unique element of the other.

Cardinality

We say sets \(A\) and \(B\) have the same cardinality if there exists a bijection

\[ f:A\to B. \]

We write

\[ |A|=|B| \]

to indicate that \(A\) and \(B\) have the same cardinality.

Finite Sets

Definition. A set \(A\) is finite if \(A=\emptyset\), or if

\[ |A|=\bigl|\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\bigr| \]

for some \(n\in\mathbb{N}\). In the latter case, we write

\[ |A|=n. \]

We write

\[ |\emptyset|=0. \]

For a finite set \(A\), it is also common to write

\[ \#A \]

for \(|A|\).

Comparing Cardinalities

Definition. For sets \(A\) and \(B\), define

\[ |A|\leq |B| \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \text{there exists an injection }f:A\to B. \]

Similarly, define

\[ |A|<|B| \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad |A|\leq |B|\text{ and }|A|\neq |B|. \]
Example

Given \(A\subset B\subset\mathbb R\), define the function

\[ f_{A,B}:A\to B,\qquad f_{A,B}(x)=x. \]

Observe that \(f_{A,B}\) is always injective.

Since

\[ \{1,2,3\}\subset\mathbb N\subset\mathbb Z\subset\mathbb Q\subset\mathbb R, \]

we may use \(f_{A,B}\) with suitably chosen \(A\) and \(B\) to conclude

\[ |\{1,2,3\}|\leq|\mathbb N|\leq|\mathbb Z|\leq|\mathbb Q|\leq|\mathbb R|. \]
├── cantor-schroder-bernstein/
Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem

If

\[ |A|\leq |B| \]

and

\[ |B|\leq |A|, \]

then

\[ |A|=|B|. \]

Equivalently, if there exists an injection \(f:A\to B\) and an injection \(g:B\to A\), then there exists a bijection between \(A\) and \(B\).

Practical Remark

Finding an explicit bijection between two sets may be difficult.

The Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein theorem lets us instead prove that two sets have the same cardinality by finding an injection in each direction.

Example

We claim

\[ |[0,1]|=|(0,1)|. \]

Define

\[ f:(0,1)\to[0,1],\qquad f(x)=x, \]

noting that \(f\) is injective. Hence

\[ |(0,1)|\leq |[0,1]|. \]

Conversely, define

\[ g:[0,1]\to(0,1),\qquad g(x)=\frac{x+1}{3}. \]

Then \(g\) is injective, with

\[ g([0,1])=\left[\frac13,\frac23\right]\subsetneq(0,1). \]

Hence

\[ |[0,1]|\leq |(0,1)|. \]

Therefore, by Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein,

\[ |[0,1]|=|(0,1)|. \]
For comparison: an explicit bijection

One explicit bijection \(h:[0,1]\to(0,1)\) is

\[ h(x)= \begin{cases} \frac12, & x=0,\\[2mm] \frac13, & x=1,\\[2mm] \frac{1}{n+2}, & x=\frac1n,\quad n\geq 2,\\[2mm] x, & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases} \]

This shifts the sequence

\[ 0,\ 1,\ \frac12,\ \frac13,\ \frac14,\ldots \]

into

\[ \frac12,\ \frac13,\ \frac14,\ \frac15,\ \frac16,\ldots \]

and fixes every other point.

├── countability/
Countably infinite sets

A set \(A\) is countably infinite if

\[ |A|=|\mathbb{N}|. \]

That is, \(A\) is countably infinite if there exists a bijection

\[ f:\mathbb{N}\to A. \]
Countable and uncountable sets

A set \(A\) is countable if it is either

  • finite, or
  • countably infinite.

If \(A\) is not countable, then \(A\) is uncountable.

We will see later that \(|\mathbb{N}|<|\mathbb{R}|\); i.e., \(\mathbb{R}\) is uncountable.

Continuum Hypothesis

A natural question is:

Is there a set \(A\) whose cardinality lies strictly between that of \(\mathbb{N}\) and \(\mathbb{R}\)?

That is, can we have

\[ |\mathbb{N}|<|A|<|\mathbb{R}|? \]

The Continuum Hypothesis says that no such set exists.

└── examples/
Example

(i) Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and define \(\{x_n\}\) by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=c. \]

Then

\[ \lim x_n=c. \]

(ii) Define \(\{x_n\}\) by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=3+\frac{(-1)^n}{n}. \]

Then

\[ \lim x_n=3. \]

Proof.

(i) Let \(\epsilon>0\) and choose \(N=1\).

For every \(n\geq N\),

\[ |x_n-c|=|c-c|=0<\epsilon. \]

Therefore \(\lim x_n=c\).

(ii) Let \(\epsilon>0\).

By the Archimedean property, choose \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ N>\frac1\epsilon. \]

Then for every \(n\geq N\),

\[ |x_n-3|=\left|\frac{(-1)^n}{n}\right|=\frac1n\leq\frac1N<\epsilon. \]

Therefore \(\lim x_n=3\).

├── size-order-and-completeness/
├── power-sets/
Power Sets

If \(A\) is a set, define its power set by

\[ \mathcal{P}(A)=\{B:B\subset A\}. \]

That is, \(\mathcal{P}(A)\) is the set of all subsets of \(A\).

Examples

If \(A=\emptyset\), then

\[ \mathcal{P}(A)=\{\emptyset\}. \]

If \(A=\{1\}\), then

\[ \mathcal{P}(A)=\{\emptyset,\{1\}\}. \]

If \(A=\{1,2\}\), then

\[ \mathcal{P}(A)=\{\emptyset,\{1\},\{2\},\{1,2\}\}. \]

In general, if \(|A|=n\), then

\[ |\mathcal{P}(A)|=2^n. \]

This is why \(\mathcal{P}(A)\) is called the power set of \(A\).

Why \(|\mathcal{P}(A)|=2^n\)

We prove this by induction on \(n=|A|\), starting at \(n=1\).

Base case: \(n=1\). If \(|A|=1\), write \(A=\{a\}\). Then

\[ \mathcal{P}(A)=\{\emptyset,\{a\}\},\qquad |\mathcal{P}(A)|=2=2^1. \]

Thus the statement holds when \(n=1\).

Induction hypothesis: Assume that every set \(B\) with \(|B|=n\) satisfies

\[ |\mathcal{P}(B)|=2^n. \]

Inductive step:

Recall. To establish the inductive step, we assume the statement holds for \(n\) (the induction hypothesis) and use this to prove it holds for \(n+1\).

Let \(|A|=n+1\). Choose \(a\in A\) and set

\[ B=A\setminus\{a\}. \]

Then \(|B|=n\), so by the induction hypothesis,

\[ |\mathcal{P}(B)|=2^n. \]

Every subset of \(A\) either does not contain \(a\), or contains \(a\). Hence

\[ \mathcal{P}(A) =\mathcal{P}(B)\cup\{C\cup\{a\}:C\in\mathcal{P}(B)\}. \]

These two collections are disjoint and have the same size, namely \(|\mathcal{P}(B)|\). Therefore

\[ |\mathcal{P}(A)|=2|\mathcal{P}(B)|=2\cdot 2^n=2^{n+1}. \]

Conclusion: By mathematical induction,

\[ |\mathcal{P}(A)|=2^{|A|} \]

for every finite nonempty set \(A\). Together with the case \(A=\emptyset\) above, this proves the formula for every finite set \(A\).

├── cantors-theorem/
Question

So how many cardinalities are there?

We know the finite cardinalities, and

\[ |\mathbb{N}|\leq|\mathbb{R}|. \]

Are there more?

Cantor's Theorem

Theorem. For every set \(A\),

\[ |A|<|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

Remark. Every set has a strictly larger power set. In particular, there is no largest cardinality.

Thus there are “infinitely many infinities.”

Proof. If \(A=\emptyset\), then

\[ \mathcal{P}(A)=\{\emptyset\}, \]

and hence

\[ |A|=0<1=|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

Assume now that \(A\neq\emptyset\).

The map

\[ A\to\mathcal{P}(A),\qquad x\mapsto\{x\}, \]

is injective, so

\[ |A|\leq|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

Suppose, for contradiction, that

\[ |A|=|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

Recall. The goal is now to derive a mathematical absurdity—that is, a contradiction—from this assumption, and thereby conclude that the assumption is false.

Since we already know \(|A|\leq|\mathcal{P}(A)|\), this would establish

\[ |A|<|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

By the definition of equality of cardinality,

\[ |A|=|\mathcal{P}(A)| \]

implies that there exists a bijection

\[ g:A\to\mathcal{P}(A). \]

Define \(B\in\mathcal{P}(A)\) by

\[ B=\{x\in A:x\notin g(x)\}. \]

Since \(g:A\to\mathcal{P}(A)\) is surjective and \(B\in\mathcal{P}(A)\),

\[ \exists\, b\in A\text{ such that }g(b)=B. \]

But,

\[ b\in B \iff b\notin g(b). \]

Since \(g(b)=B\),

\[ b\in B \iff b\notin B, \]

which is impossible. This is the desired contradiction.

We already proved that

\[ |A|\leq|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

The contradiction shows that

\[ |A|\neq|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]

Therefore,

\[ |A|<|\mathcal{P}(A)|. \]
Consequences

Applying Cantor's theorem repeatedly gives

\[ |\mathbb{N}|<|\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})| <|\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}))|<\cdots. \]

Thus there are infinitely many different infinite cardinalities.

Question. How many cardinalities are there? Countably many? Uncountably many? Is this question even well-defined?

Recall. The continuum hypothesis asks whether there is a cardinality strictly between \(|\mathbb{N}|\) and \(|\mathbb{R}|\). We will later show that

\[ |\mathbb{R}|=|\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})|. \]
├── ordered-sets/
Ordered Sets

We now formalize the notion of an ordering. In this framework, \(\mathbb{Q}\) lacks an important property, which suggests the need for a larger set, namely \(\mathbb{R}\).

Ordered Pairs

Definition (Kuratowski). The ordered pair \((a,b)\) is defined by

\[ (a,b)=\{\{a\},\{a,b\}\}. \]

Then

\[ (a,b)=(c,d)\iff a=c\text{ and }b=d. \]
Cartesian Products

Definition. The Cartesian product of sets \(A\) and \(B\) is defined by

\[ A\times B=\{(a,b):a\in A,\ b\in B\}. \]
Relations

A relation from \(A\) to \(B\) is a subset

\[ R\subset A\times B. \]

We write

\[ aRb \]

when

\[ (a,b)\in R. \]

If \(A=B\), then \(R\) is called a relation on \(A\).

Functions

A function

\[ f:A\to B \]

is a relation

\[ f\subset A\times B \]

such that for every \(a\in A\), there is exactly one \(b\in B\) with

\[ (a,b)\in f. \]

Notation. Since a function is a relation,

\[ f(a)=b \iff a\,f\,b \iff (a,b)\in f. \]
Orders

Definition. An order \(<\) on \(S\) is a relation on \(S\) such that:

  1. Trichotomy: For every \(x,y\in S\), exactly one of \[ x<y,\qquad x=y,\qquad y<x \] holds.
  2. Transitivity: If \(x<y\) and \(y<z\), then \[ x<z. \]

The pair \((S,<)\) is called an ordered set.

Notation. We write \(b>a\) to mean \(a<b\).

Examples

\((\mathbb{Z},<)\) is an ordered set, where

\[ m<n\iff n-m\in\mathbb{N}. \]

\((\mathbb{Q},<)\) is an ordered set, where

\[ p<q\iff q-p=\frac{m}{n} \]

for some \(m,n\in\mathbb{N}\).

\(\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}\) can be ordered using the lexicographic order, also called the dictionary order:

\[ (q,r)<(s,t) \iff q<s,\text{ or }q=s\text{ and }r<t. \]
Non-example

Consider \(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})\). Define a relation \(R\) on \(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})\) by

\[ A\,R\,B\iff A\subsetneq B. \]

For example, let

\[ A=\{1\}, \qquad B=\{2\}. \]

Then neither

\[ A\,R\,B \]

nor

\[ B\,R\,A \]

holds. Thus \(R\) fails trichotomy, so \(R\) is not an order in the sense defined above.

Note. \(R\) is, however, a strict partial order.

├── bounds/
Bounds, Supremum, and Infimum

Let \((S,<)\) be an ordered set and \(E\subset S\).

Definition. Define the relation \(\leq\) on \(S\) by

\[ x\leq y\iff x<y\text{ or }x=y. \]
Upper and Lower Bounds

Definition. \(E\) is bounded above if there exists \(b\in S\) such that

\[ x\leq b \]

for every \(x\in E\). Such a \(b\) is called an upper bound of \(E\).

Similarly, \(E\) is bounded below if there exists \(c\in S\) such that

\[ x\geq c \]

for every \(x\in E\). Such a \(c\) is called a lower bound of \(E\).

Supremum and Infimum

Definition. An element \(b_0\in S\) is the least upper bound, or supremum, of \(E\) if:

  1. \(b_0\) is an upper bound of \(E\).
  2. If \(b\) is any upper bound of \(E\), then \(b_0\leq b\).

We write

\[ b_0=\sup E. \]

Similarly, an element \(c_0\in S\) is the greatest lower bound, or infimum, of \(E\) if:

  1. \(c_0\) is a lower bound of \(E\).
  2. If \(c\) is any lower bound of \(E\), then \(c\leq c_0\).

We write

\[ c_0=\inf E. \]
Examples

In \((\mathbb{Z},<)\), if

\[ E=\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}, \]

then

\[ \inf E=-2,\qquad \sup E=2. \]

The supremum or infimum need not belong to \(E\). In \((\mathbb{Q},<)\), if

\[ E=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:0\leq q<1\}, \]

then

\[ \inf E=0\in E,\qquad \sup E=1\notin E. \]

A supremum or infimum need not exist. In \((\mathbb{Z},<)\), if

\[ E=\mathbb{N}, \]

then

\[ \inf E=1, \]

but \(\sup E\) does not exist.

Notation. In this situation, one often writes

\[ \sup E=+\infty \]

to indicate that \(E\) is unbounded above. Here \(+\infty\notin\mathbb{Z}\), so this is not a supremum in \((\mathbb{Z},<)\).

├── least-upper-bound-property/
Least Upper Bound Property

Let \((S,<)\) be an ordered set.

We say that \(S\) has the least upper bound property if every nonempty set \(E\subset S\) that is bounded above has a supremum in \(S\).

Example

\((\mathbb{N},<)\) has the least upper bound property.

Let \(E\subset\mathbb{N}\) be nonempty and bounded above. Define the set of upper bounds

\[ U=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:x\leq n\text{ for every }x\in E\}. \]

Since \(E\) is bounded above, \(U\neq\emptyset\).

By the well-ordering property of \(\mathbb{N}\), \(U\) has a least element \(m\).

Therefore \(m\) is the least upper bound of \(E\), so

\[ m=\sup E. \]

Thus \((\mathbb{N},<)\) has the least upper bound property.

We will now show that \((\mathbb{Q},<)\) does not have the least upper bound property.

└── q-does-not-have-lub-property/
\(\mathbb{Q}\) Does Not Have the Least Upper Bound Property

We have already proved that

\[ \sqrt{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}. \]

Let

\[ E=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:q>0,\ q^2<2\}. \]

Since \(1\in E\), the set \(E\) is nonempty. Also, \(2\) is an upper bound of \(E\).

Theorem. If \(x=\sup E\) in \(\mathbb{Q}\), then

\[ x^2=2. \]

Proof. Since \(1\in E=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:q>0,\ q^2<2\}\),

\[ x\geq 1. \]

Suppose, for contradiction, that \(x^2<2\).

Set

\[ h=\frac{2-x^2}{2x+1}>0. \]

Goal. Prove \(x+h\in E\), contradicting that \(x\) is an upper bound of \(E\).

Since \(x\in\mathbb{Q}\), we have \(h\in\mathbb{Q}\), and hence \(x+h\in\mathbb{Q}\).

Since \(x\geq1\), we also have \(h<1\).

Thus

\[ \begin{aligned} (x+h)^2 &=x^2+h(2x+h)\\ &<x^2+h(2x+1)\\ &=2. \end{aligned} \]

Since \(x+h\in\mathbb{Q}\), \(x+h>0\), and \((x+h)^2<2\), we have \(x+h\in E\).

But \(h>0\) gives \(x+h>x\), contradicting that \(x\) is an upper bound of \(E\).

Therefore

\[ x^2\geq2. \]

Now suppose, for contradiction, that \(x^2>2\).

Set

\[ h=\frac{x^2-2}{2x}>0. \]

Goal. Show that \(x-h\) is an upper bound of \(E\), contradicting that \(x=\sup E\).

Since \(x\in\mathbb{Q}\), we have \(h\in\mathbb{Q}\), and hence \(x-h\in\mathbb{Q}\).

We have

\[ x-h=\frac{x^2+2}{2x}>0 \]

and

\[ (x-h)^2=x^2-2xh+h^2=2+h^2>2. \]

Now let \(q\in E\).

Since \(q>0\), \(q^2<2\), and \(x-h>0\), we have

\[ q^2<2<(x-h)^2, \]

and therefore

\[ q<x-h. \]

Hence \(x-h\) is an upper bound of \(E\).

But \(h>0\) gives \(x-h<x\), contradicting that \(x=\sup E\).

Putting the two contradictions together, we have

\[ x^2\geq2 \qquad\text{and}\qquad x^2\leq2. \]

Hence

\[ x^2=2. \]

Corollary. \((\mathbb{Q},<)\) does not have the least upper bound property.

Goal. Show by counterexample that there exists a subset of \(\mathbb{Q}\) with no least upper bound.

Proof. Let

\[ E=\{q\in\mathbb{Q}:q>0,\ q^2<2\}. \]

Suppose \(x=\sup E\) for some \(x\in\mathbb{Q}\).

By the previous theorem, \(x^2=2\).

Since \(1\in E\) and \(x=\sup E\) is an upper bound of \(E\), we have \(x\geq1\).

Hence \(x=\sqrt{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}\).

Therefore no \(x\in\mathbb{Q}\) can equal \(\sup E\), so \(E\) has no supremum in \(\mathbb{Q}\).

Hence \((\mathbb{Q},<)\) does not have the least upper bound property.

├── characterization-of-the-real-numbers/
├── fields/
Goal: Formally Characterize the Real Numbers

Before doing this, we need to formalize its underlying algebraic structure.

Note. One may formally construct \(\mathbb{R}\), but we will not do that here.

Examples of constructions are Dedekind cuts and Cauchy completion.

Fields

A field is a set \(F\) equipped with two binary operations:

\[ +:F\times F\to F,\qquad (x,y)\mapsto x+y, \]

and

\[ \cdot:F\times F\to F,\qquad (x,y)\mapsto x\cdot y. \]

satisfying the following axioms of Addition, Multiplication and Distributivity.

Remark. Note that \(F\) does not have to have anything to do with numbers.

What follows is a formalization of “addition” and “multiplication”.

Addition

Let \(x,y,z\in F\).

A1) Closure under addition:

\[ x+y\in F. \]

A2) Commutativity:

\[ x+y=y+x. \]

A3) Associativity:

\[ (x+y)+z=x+(y+z). \]

A4) There exists \(0\in F\) such that

\[ 0+x=x=x+0. \]

Such an element \(0\) can be shown to be unique and we call it an additive identity.

A5) For every \(x\in F\), there exists \(y\in F\) such that

\[ x+y=0. \]

Such an element \(y\) can be shown to be unique. We call it an additive inverse and write \(-x\) for it.

Multiplication

Let \(x,y,z\in F\).

M1) Closure under multiplication:

\[ x\cdot y\in F. \]

M2) Commutativity:

\[ x\cdot y=y\cdot x. \]

M3) Associativity:

\[ (x\cdot y)\cdot z=x\cdot(y\cdot z). \]

M4) There exists \(1\in F\setminus\{0\}\) such that

\[ 1\cdot x=x=x\cdot 1. \]

Such an element \(1\) can be shown to be unique and we call it the multiplicative identity.

M5) For every \(x\in F\setminus\{0\}\), there exists \(y\in F\) such that

\[ x\cdot y=1. \]

Such an element \(y\) can be shown to be unique. We call it a multiplicative inverse and we write \(x^{-1}\) for it.

Distributivity

Let \(x,y,z\in F\).

D) Distributivity:

\[ (x+y)z=xz+yz. \]

Remark. Distributivity is a compatibility between the two operations \(+\) and \(\cdot\).

├── field-examples/
Example 1
\[ \mathbb{Z}_2=\{0,1\}. \]

Addition in \(\mathbb{Z}_2\) is defined by:

+01
001
110

Multiplication in \(\mathbb{Z}_2\) is defined by:

·01
000
101
Example 2
\[ \mathbb{Z}_3=\{0,1,2\}. \]

Addition in \(\mathbb{Z}_3\) is defined by:

+012
0012
1120
2201

Multiplication in \(\mathbb{Z}_3\) is defined by:

·012
0000
1012
2021
Non-Example

\(\mathbb{Z}\) is not a field because M5 fails: multiplicative inverses do not generally exist in \(\mathbb{Z}\).

├── zero-is-absorbing/
Zero is Absorbing for Multiplication

Theorem. Let \(F\) be a field. If \(x\in F\), then

\[ 0\cdot x=0. \]

Proof.

\[ \begin{aligned} 0 &=0\cdot x-0\cdot x &&\text{(additive inverse)}\\ &=(0+0)\cdot x-0\cdot x &&\text{(additive identity)}\\ &=0\cdot x+0\cdot x-0\cdot x &&\text{(distributivity)}\\ &=0\cdot x &&\text{(associativity, additive inverse, additive identity)}. \end{aligned} \]

Therefore, \(0\cdot x=0\).

├── ordered-fields/
Ordered Fields

Let \(F\) be a field and let \(<\) be an order on \(F\). We call \(F\) an ordered field if:

i) For all \(x,y,z\in F\),

\[ x<y \implies x+z<y+z. \]

ii) If \(x>0\) and \(y>0\), then

\[ xy>0. \]

Terminology. If \(x>0\), we say \(x\) is positive. If \(x\geq 0\), we say \(x\) is non-negative.

If \(x<0\), we say \(x\) is negative. If \(x\leq 0\), we say \(x\) is non-positive.

Proposition 1. \(\mathbb{Q}\), with the order defined by

\[ p<q \iff q-p=\frac{n}{m} \quad\text{for some }m,n\in\mathbb{N}, \]

is an ordered field.

Proof. We have already seen that this relation is an order on \(\mathbb{Q}\). It remains to verify that the order is compatible with addition and multiplication.

Suppose \(x<y\). Then, by definition,

\[ y-x=\frac{n}{m} \]

for some \(m,n\in\mathbb{N}\). For any \(z\in\mathbb{Q}\),

\[ (y+z)-(x+z)=y-x=\frac{n}{m}. \]

Therefore, by the definition of the order,

\[ x+z<y+z. \]

Now suppose \(x>0\) and \(y>0\). By definition of the order, there exist \(a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ x=\frac{a}{b}, \qquad y=\frac{c}{d}. \]

Hence

\[ xy=\frac{ac}{bd}. \]

Since \(ac,bd\in\mathbb{N}\), the definition of the order gives

\[ xy>0. \]

Thus \(\mathbb{Q}\) is an ordered field.

Proposition 2. \(\mathbb{Z}_2\) is not an ordered field.

Proof. Suppose, for contradiction, that there is an order \(<\) on \(\mathbb{Z}_2=\{0,1\}\) that makes \(\mathbb{Z}_2\) an ordered field.

Since \(0\neq1\), trichotomy implies that either

\[ 0<1 \qquad\text{or}\qquad 1<0. \]

Recall: trichotomy for \(<\) means exactly one of \(x<y\), \(x=y\), or \(y<x\) holds.

Suppose first that

\[ 0<1. \]

The following shows \(0<1 \implies 1<0\), contradicting trichotomy.

If instead

\[ 1<0, \]

the following shows \(1<0 \implies 0<1\), again contradicting trichotomy.

Since we have shown

\[ \mathbb{Z}_2\text{ is an ordered field} \implies \bigl(0<1 \iff 1<0\bigr), \]

we achieve a contradiction, and so \(\mathbb{Z}_2\) cannot be an ordered field.

Question. Do there exist any ordered finite fields?

├── elementary-properties/

Theorem. Let \(F\) be a field and let \(x,y\in F\). Then

\[ x(-y)=-(xy) \qquad\text{and}\qquad -x=(-1)x. \]

Proof. Since

\[ x(-y)+xy=x(-y+y)=x\cdot0=0, \]

\(x(-y)\) is the additive inverse of \(xy\), so \(x(-y)=-(xy)\).

Similarly,

\[ (-1)x+x=(-1+1)x=0\cdot x=0, \]

so \((-1)x\) is the additive inverse of \(x\), hence \(-x=(-1)x\).

Theorem. Let \((F,<)\) be an ordered field. For \(x\in F\), it holds that

\[ 0<x \iff -x<0. \]

Proof.

We show \(-x<0 \implies 0<x\). The other direction is similar.

Theorem. Let \((F,<)\) be an ordered field and let \(x,y\in F\). If \(0<x\) and \(y<0\), then

\[ xy<0. \]

Proof.

Preceding theorem gives \(y<0 \implies 0<-y\).

By definition of ordered field and preceding theorem, we have:

├── lub-and-glb/
Least Upper Bounds and Greatest Lower Bounds

Let \((S,<)\) be an ordered set.

Recall:

  • \((S,<)\) has the least upper bound property (lub) when
    \(E\subset S\) bounded above and nonempty \(\implies\) \(E\) has a supremum in \(S\).
  • \((S,<)\) has the greatest lower bound property (glb) when
    \(E\subset S\) bounded below and nonempty \(\implies\) \(E\) has an infimum in \(S\).

Remark. In fact, lub and glb are equivalent for any ordered set. For an ordered field, there is a particularly simple proof using negation, which we give below.

Theorem. Let \((F,<)\) be an ordered field. Then \(F\) has the least upper bound property if and only if \(F\) has the greatest lower bound property.

Proof. We prove \({\rm lub} \implies {\rm glb}\). The other direction is similar.

LUB \(\Rightarrow\) GLB

Suppose \((F,<)\) has the least upper bound property.

Let \(A\subset F\) be nonempty and bounded below.

Define

\[ B=\{-x:x\in A\}. \]
------)------(-------
\(B\) \(A\)

Plan:

  1. show \(B\) is bounded above
  2. use lub \(\Rightarrow \sup B\) exists
  3. show \(-\sup B\) is a lower bound of \(A\)
  4. Show \(\inf A\) exists and \(\inf A=-\sup B\).

Let \(a\) be a lower bound of \(A\).

We show \(-a\) is an upper bound of \(B\) and hence \(B\) is bounded above.

Thus, letting \(b\in B\) be arbitrary, we aim to show \(b\le -a\).

By the least upper bound property,

\[ c=\sup B \]

exists in \(F\). (Note: \(A\neq\emptyset\implies B\neq\emptyset\).)

We show that \(-c\) is a lower bound of \(A\).

Thus, letting \(x\in A\) be arbitrary, we aim to show \(-c\le x\).

Now let \(a\) be any lower bound for \(A\).

We show that \(a\le -c\).

Since \(-c\) is a lower bound of \(A\), this establishes \(\inf A\) exists and \(\inf A=-c=-\sup B\).

├── real-numbers/
Characterizing the Real Numbers

Theorem. There exists an ordered field \(\mathbb{R}\) such that \(\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}\), such that \(\mathbb{Q}\) is a subfield of \(\mathbb{R}\), and such that \(\mathbb{R}\) has the least upper bound property.

Definition. Let \(F\) be a field and let \(K\subset F\). We call \(K\) a subfield of \(F\) if \(K\) is itself a field using the same addition and multiplication as \(F\).

Essential uniqueness. Any ordered field containing \(\mathbb{Q}\) as a subfield and having the least upper bound property can be put in one-to-one correspondence with \(\mathbb{R}\) in a way that preserves addition, multiplication, and order.

└── square-root-of-two/
Existence and Uniqueness of \(\sqrt{2}\)

Theorem. There exists a unique \(r\in\mathbb{R}\) such that

\[ r>0 \]

and

\[ r^2=2. \]

Proof. We break the proof into two parts: Existence and Uniqueness.

Existence follows from \(\mathbb{R}\) having lub.

Uniqueness follows from a direct calculation.

Existence

Define

\[ \widetilde{E}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x>0\text{ and }x^2<2\}. \]

\(\widetilde{E}\) is bounded above and nonempty; e.g., \(1\in\widetilde{E}\) and \(2\) is an upper bound.

Since \(\mathbb{R}\) has the least upper bound property,

\[ r=\sup\widetilde{E} \]

exists.

As in the corresponding argument for \(\mathbb{Q}\), one shows that \(r>0\) and \(r^2=2\).

Uniqueness

Suppose \(s>0\) also satisfies \(s^2=2\).

Then \(r+s>0\) and

\[ 0=r^2-s^2=(r+s)(r-s). \]

Since \(r+s>0\), we have \(r+s\neq0\). Multiplying by \((r+s)^{-1}\) gives \(r-s=0\), i.e., \(r=s\).

├── archimedean-property-and-density-of-the-rationals/
├── archimedean-property/
Archimedean Property

Theorem. If \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(x>0\), then there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ nx>y. \]

Remark. The ordered-field axioms alone do not rule out positive infinitesimals \(\varepsilon\) for which

\[ \{n\varepsilon:n\in\mathbb{N}\} \]

is bounded.

\(\mathbb{R}\) satisfies both the Archimedean property and density of \(\mathbb{Q}\) because it is an ordered field with the least upper bound property.

An ordered field for which neither property holds is the hyperreal field \(\mathbb{R}^\ast\), a “field extension” of \(\mathbb{R}\) admitting “infinitesimals” and “infinities.”

There exists a positive infinitesimal \(\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^\ast\) such that

\[ n\varepsilon<1 \qquad\text{for every }n\in\mathbb{N}. \]

Hence \(\{n\varepsilon:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\) is bounded.

Proof of the Archimedean Property

Archimedean Property. If \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(x>0\), then there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ nx>y. \]

Suppose \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(x>0\).

We will show there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ n>\frac{y}{x}. \]

Suppose, for contradiction, that this is not the case.

Then, for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ n\leq\frac{y}{x}. \]

Note. This implies \(\mathbb{N}\) is bounded above. Since \(\mathbb{R}\) satisfies lub, \(a=\sup\mathbb{N}\) exists in \(\mathbb{R}\).

We use \(a=\sup\mathbb{N}\) exists in \(\mathbb{R}\) \(\implies\) \(a-1\) not an upper bound of \(\mathbb{N}\) to arrive at a contradiction:

Since

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ n\leq\frac{y}{x} \]

results in a contradiction, we conclude \(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t. \(nx>y\), as desired.

└── density-of-the-rationals/
Density of the Rationals

For \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(x<y\), there exists \(r\in\mathbb{R}\) such that \(x<r<y\).

For example, take

\[ r=\frac{x+y}{2}. \]

Can we always find \(r\in\mathbb{Q}\) such that \(x<r<y\)?

In particular: can we always find a rational between any two irrationals, no matter how close they are?

The answer is yes:

Theorem

If \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(x<y\), then there exists \(r\in\mathbb{Q}\) such that

\[ x<r<y. \]

Remark. For the hyperreal field \(\mathbb{R}^\ast\),

\[ \nexists q\in\mathbb{Q}\text{ such that }0<q<\varepsilon, \]

so \(\mathbb{Q}\) is not dense in \(\mathbb{R}^\ast\).

Proof of the Density of \(\mathbb{Q}\)

Density of \(\mathbb{Q}\). If \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(x<y\), then there exists \(r\in\mathbb{Q}\) such that

\[ x<r<y. \]

Suppose \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(x<y\).

We consider three cases:

Case 1: \(0\leq x<y\).

Case 2: \(x<0<y\).

Case 3: \(x<y\leq0\).

Case 1: \(0\leq x<y\).

Plan

1. Choose the denominator \(n\) so that rationals with denominator \(n\) are spaced more closely than \(x\) and \(y\). By the Archimedean property, choose \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \frac{1}{n}<y-x, \]

equivalently,

\[ x+\frac{1}{n}<y. \]

2. Choose the least rational of the form \(m/n\) that is greater than \(x\). By well-ordering, choose the least \(m\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ x<\frac{m}{n}. \]

Minimality of \(m\) gives

\[ \frac{m-1}{n}\leq x<\frac{m}{n}, \]

so

\[ x<\frac{m}{n}\leq x+\frac{1}{n}. \]

3. Show that this rational is less than \(y\). Since

\[ x<\frac{m}{n}\leq x+\frac{1}{n}<y, \]

we obtain

\[ x<\frac{m}{n}<y. \]

We first show

\[ x+\frac{1}{n}<y \]

which is equivalent to

\[ nx+1<ny. \]

Next we show there exists a least \(m\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(x<m/n\).

Let

\[ S=\{k\in\mathbb{N}:nx<k\}. \]

Archimedean property \(\implies S\neq\emptyset\) since \(\exists l\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t. \(l>nx\). (here "\(x=1\)" and "\(y=nx\)")

Well ordering property of \(\mathbb{N}\implies S\) has least element \(m\).

Hence \(m\in S\), so \(nx<m\). Since \(n>0\),

\[ x<\frac{m}{n}. \]

We claim that

\[ m-1\leq nx. \]

If \(m=1\), then \(m-1=0\leq nx\) since \(x\geq0\).

If \(m>1\), then \(m-1\in\mathbb{N}\).

Since \(m\) is the least element of \(S\), \(m-1\notin S\), so again \(m-1\leq nx\).

Since \(m-1\leq nx\), we have \(m\leq nx+1<ny\).

Since \(n>0\),

\[ \frac{m}{n}<y. \]

But \(x<m/n\).

Since \(m/n\in\mathbb{Q}\), we conclude there exists \(q\in\mathbb{Q}\) such that

\[ x<q<y. \]

Case 2: \(x<0<y\).

Take \(r=0\in\mathbb{Q}\).

Then \(x<r<y\).

Case 3: \(x<y\leq0\).

We may reduce Case 3 to Case 1 as follows.

Therefore, whenever \(x<y\), there exists \(r\in\mathbb{Q}\) such that \(x<r<y\).

├── properties-of-the-supremum/
├── a-supremum-example/
A Supremum Example

Theorem.

Let

\[ E=\left\{1-\frac1n:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}. \]

Then

\[ \sup E=1. \]

Proof.

Plan

  1. Show a natural candidate \(s\) for the supremum is an upper bound.
  2. Show that any upper bound \(x\) satisfies \(s\leq x\).
  3. Conclude the supremum is \(s\).

1. We show \(1\) is an upper bound of \(E=\{1-1/n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\); i.e.,

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad 1-\frac{1}{n}\leq1. \]

Useful proof strategy. When trying to prove an inequality, sometimes it is easier to start with the desired inequality and reverse engineer until you arrive at something known to be true.

Indeed, the reverse engineering

\[ 1-\frac{1}{n}\leq1 \iff 1\leq1+\frac{1}{n} \iff 0\leq\frac{1}{n} \iff 0<n \]

gives us

\[ n\in\mathbb{N} \implies 0<n \implies 0\leq\frac{1}{n} \implies 1\leq1+\frac{1}{n} \implies 1-\frac{1}{n}\leq1. \]

2. We now show that if \(x\) is an upper bound of \(E=\{1-1/n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\), then \(1\leq x\).

For sake of contradiction suppose \(x<1\), and hence \(0<1-x\).

Recall the Archimedean property: if \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(a>0\), then \(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t. \(na>b\).

Thus \(\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ 1<n(1-x). \]

But

\[ 1<n(1-x) \implies \frac{1}{n}<1-x \implies x<1-\frac{1}{n}. \]

Since \(1-\frac{1}{n}\in E\), this contradicts the assumption that \(x\) is an upper bound of \(E\).

Therefore, if \(x\) is an upper bound of \(E\), then \(1\leq x\).

3. Since step 1 shows that \(1\) is an upper bound of \(E\), and step 2 shows that every upper bound \(x\) of \(E\) satisfies \(1\leq x\), we conclude

\[ \sup E=1. \]
├── characterizing-the-supremum/
Characterizing the Supremum

We now prove some results about supremums and infimums that will make them easier to use.

Theorem. Suppose \(S\subset\mathbb{R}\) is nonempty and bounded above. Then \(x=\sup S\) if and only if:

  1. \(x\) is an upper bound for \(S\).
  2. For every \(\varepsilon>0\), there exists \(y\in S\) such that \[ x-\varepsilon<y\leq x. \]

The proof is left as an exercise.

├── suprema-under-translation-and-positive-scaling/
Suprema Under Translation and Positive Scaling

Notation. For \(x\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(A\subset\mathbb{R}\), define

\[ x+A:=\{x+a:a\in A\}, \] \[ xA:=\{xa:a\in A\}. \]

Theorem. If \(x\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(A\) is bounded above, then \(x+A\) is bounded above and

\[ \sup(x+A)=x+\sup A. \]

If \(x>0\) and \(A\) is bounded above, then \(xA\) is bounded above and

\[ \sup(xA)=x\sup A. \]

Proof.

Plan

1. Show that \(x+\sup A\) is an upper bound of \(x+A\).

2. For \(x>0\), show that \(x\sup A\) is an upper bound of \(xA\).

3. Use the characterization of the supremum above to conclude that these upper bounds are in fact the corresponding suprema.

1. We show \(x+\sup A\) is an upper bound of \(x+A\).

Every point in \(x+A\) is, by definition, of the form \(x+a\) for some \(a\in A\).

For such \(a\),

\[ a\leq\sup A, \]

so

\[ x+a\leq x+\sup A. \]

Thus \(x+\sup A\) is an upper bound for \(x+A\).

2. We now show \(x\sup A\) is an upper bound of \(xA\).

For every \(a\in A\),

\[ a\leq\sup A. \]

Since \(x>0\),

\[ xa\leq x\sup A. \]

Thus \(x\sup A\) is an upper bound for \(xA\).

3. We now use the characterization of suprema above to show each upper bound is a supremum.

Recall the characterization of the supremum.

If \(M\) is an upper bound for a set \(E\), and if for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there exists \(y\in E\) such that

\[ M-\varepsilon<y\leq M, \]

then \(M=\sup E\).

3a. We show \(x+\sup A=\sup(x+A)\).

Let \(\varepsilon>0\).

Since \(\sup A\) is the supremum of \(A\), there exists \(y\in A\) such that

\[ \sup A-\varepsilon<y\leq\sup A. \]

Adding \(x\) gives

\[ (x+\sup A)-\varepsilon<x+y\leq x+\sup A. \]

Therefore, by the characterization of the supremum,

\[ \sup(x+A)=x+\sup A. \]

3b. We show \(x\sup A=\sup(xA)\).

Now assume \(x>0\).

Let \(\varepsilon>0\).

Since \(\varepsilon/x>0\), there exists \(z\in A\) such that

\[ \sup A-\frac{\varepsilon}{x}<z\leq\sup A. \]

Multiplying by \(x\) gives

\[ x\sup A-\varepsilon<xz\leq x\sup A. \]

Therefore, by the characterization of the supremum,

\[ \sup(xA)=x\sup A. \]
└── comparing-suprema-and-infima/
Comparing Suprema and Infima

Theorem. Let \(A,B\subset\mathbb{R}\) such that for every \(x\in A\) and every \(y\in B\),

\[ x\leq y. \]

Then

\[ \sup A\leq\inf B. \]

The proof is left to the reader.

├── the-uncountability-of-the-reals/
├── outline/

Fundamental question: What is the cardinality of \(\mathbb{R}\)?

In this unit we:

  • formalize decimal representations;
  • use a diagonalization argument on decimal representations to conclude \((0,1]\) is uncountable;
  • conclude \(|\mathbb{N}|<|\mathbb{R}|\).

Recall. A set \(X\) is countable if there is a bijection \(f:X\to\mathbb{N}\).

├── decimal-representations/

Goal: formalize decimal representations of real numbers.

Observe:

\(\frac{1}{2}\)
\(=\)
\(5\cdot 10^{-1}\)
\(=\)
“0.5”
\(\frac{1}{8}\)
\(=\)
\(1\cdot 10^{-1}+2\cdot 10^{-2}+5\cdot 10^{-3}\)
\(=\)
“0.125”
\(32+\frac{1}{3}\)
\(=\)
\(3\cdot 10^{1}+2\cdot 10^{0}+3\cdot 10^{-1}+3\cdot 10^{-2}+3\cdot 10^{-3}+\ldots\)
\(=\)
“32.333…”
  • This suggests a formal way to define decimal representations for at least rational numbers.
  • What about general real numbers?
Proposition

If \(x\geq0\), then there exist \(k\in\mathbb{N}\) and digits

\[ d_j\in\{0,1,\ldots,9\}, \qquad j\leq k, \]

such that

\[ x= \sup\left\{ d_k\cdot10^k+\cdots+d_1\cdot10+d_0 +d_{-1}\cdot10^{-1}+\cdots+d_{-n}\cdot10^{-n} :n\in\mathbb{N} \right\}. \]

Proof. Take for granted.

Definition. Let \(x\geq0\).

A decimal representation of \(x\) is an expression

\[ x=d_kd_{k-1}\cdots d_1d_0.d_{-1}d_{-2}\cdots \]

where \(k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\) and \(d_j\in\{0,1,\ldots,9\}\) for every integer \(j\leq k\), are such that

\[ x= \sup\left\{ d_k\cdot10^k+\cdots+d_1\cdot10+d_0 +d_{-1}\cdot10^{-1}+\cdots+d_{-n}\cdot10^{-n} :n\in\mathbb{N} \right\}. \]

Remark.

(i) If \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(d_{-j}=0\) for \(j>N\) and \(d_{-N}\neq 0\), then we write

\[ x=d_kd_{k-1}\ldots d_1d_0.d_{-1}d_{-2}\ldots d_{-N} \]

(ii) Each decimal representation determines a unique real number, but a real number may have more than one decimal representation; e.g., \(0.999\ldots=1\).

(iii) One may define decimal representations for negative real numbers too.

(iv) Given digits \(d_{-j}\in\{0,1,\ldots,9\}\), \(j\in\mathbb{N}\), the supremum

\[ \sup\left\{ d_{-1}10^{-1}+d_{-2}10^{-2}+\cdots+d_{-n}10^{-n} :n\in\mathbb{N} \right\} \]

defines a real number in \([0,1]\).

Theorem

If \(x\in(0,1]\) then

\[ \exists!\ d_{-j}\in\{0,1,\ldots,9\},\quad j\in\mathbb{N} \]

such that

  • \(x=0.d_{-1}d_{-2}\ldots\)
  • if \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), then
\[ 0.d_{-1}\ldots d_{-n}<x\leq 0.d_{-1}\ldots d_{-n}+10^{-n} \]

We call \(0.d_{-1}d_{-2}\ldots\) the distinguished decimal representation of \(x\).

Proof. Take for granted.

Remark. This theorem does not contradict the general nonuniqueness of decimal representations: it singles out one distinguished decimal representation.

E.g., even though

\[ 0.499\ldots=0.5 \]

the following is false

\[ 0.5<0.5\leq 0.5+10^{-1} \]
└── cantors-diagonalization-argument/

Goal: show that \((0,1]\) is uncountable (and hence \(\mathbb{R}\) is uncountable).

  • Maybe this is surprising: the collection
\[ \{0.d_{-1}d_{-2}\ldots:d_{-j}\in\{0,1,\ldots,9\}\} \]

somehow “feels” countable.

  • On the contrary: we use decimal representations to show a bijection \(\mathbb{N}\to(0,1]\) cannot exist.
Theorem (Cantor)

\((0,1]\) is uncountable.

Proof sketch.

0. Show that \((0,1]\) is not finite. Consequently, if \((0,1]\) were countable, then it would be countably infinite.

1. Assume for contradiction there exists a bijection \(\mathbb{N}\to(0,1]\).

2. Make the correspondence \(n\leftrightarrow\) "nth decimal representation".

3. Diagonalize step 2 to generate a decimal representation not equal to any one from step 2.

4. Combining steps 1 and 3 furnishes a contradiction.

Proof.

0. Let

\[ a_{-j}^{(n)}= \begin{cases} 1, & \text{if }j=n,\\ 0, & \text{if }j\neq n. \end{cases} \]

Define

\[ x_n=0.a_{-1}^{(n)}a_{-2}^{(n)}\ldots. \]

Thus \(x_n=10^{-n}\).

The mapping \(\mathbb{N}\to(0,1]\) defined by \(n\mapsto x_n\) is injective, and so \((0,1]\) is infinite.

1. Suppose for contradiction \((0,1]\) is countable.

Then \(\exists\) a bijection \(x:\mathbb{N}\to(0,1]\).

2. Since we may identify each number in \((0,1]\) with its distinguished decimal representation, 1. allows us to enumerate the distinguished decimal representations of numbers in \((0,1]\):

\[ \begin{aligned} x(1)&=0.d_{-1}^{(1)}d_{-2}^{(1)}d_{-3}^{(1)}\ldots\\ x(2)&=0.d_{-1}^{(2)}d_{-2}^{(2)}d_{-3}^{(2)}\ldots\\ &\vdots\\ x(n)&=0.d_{-1}^{(n)}d_{-2}^{(n)}d_{-3}^{(n)}\ldots\\ &\vdots \end{aligned} \]

3. Define digits \(e_{-j}\) by

\[ e_{-j}= \begin{cases} 1, & d_{-j}^{(j)}\neq 1,\\ 2, & d_{-j}^{(j)}=1. \end{cases} \]

Define

\[ y= \sup\left\{ e_{-1}10^{-1}+e_{-2}10^{-2}+\cdots+e_{-n}10^{-n} :n\in\mathbb{N} \right\}. \]

Since \(e_{-j}\geq 1\), there holds

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad 0.e_{-1}\ldots e_{-n}<y\leq 0.e_{-1}\ldots e_{-n}+10^{-n}. \]

In particular, \(0.e_{-1}e_{-2}\ldots\) is the distinguished decimal representation of \(y\).

Illustration.

The diagram below depicts an example construction of \(y\).

\[ \begin{array}{c|cccccc} & d_{-1} & d_{-2} & d_{-3} & d_{-4} & d_{-5} & d_{-6} \\ \hline x(1)=0. & \boxed{3} & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 1 & \ldots\\ x(2)=0. & 4 & \boxed{1} & 8 & 9 & 0 & 5 & \ldots\\ x(3)=0. & 5 & 5 & \boxed{4} & 3 & 3 & 8 & \ldots\\ x(4)=0. & 7 & 4 & 4 & \boxed{7} & 6 & 0 & \ldots\\ x(5)=0. & 8 & 9 & 0 & 0 & \boxed{1} & 7 & \ldots\\ x(6)=0. & 1 & 2 & 2 & 2 & 3 & \boxed{8} & \ldots\\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \end{array} \]
\(\Longrightarrow\)
\[ \begin{array}{cccccc} e_{-1} & e_{-2} & e_{-3} & e_{-4} & e_{-5} & e_{-6} \\ \boxed{1} & & & & & \\ & \boxed{2} & & & & \\ & & \boxed{1} & & & \\ & & & \boxed{1} & & \\ & & & & \boxed{2} & \\ & & & & & \boxed{1} \end{array} \]
\(y=0.121121\ldots\)

Observe.

  • \(e_{-1}=1\neq 3=d_{-1}^{(1)}\implies y\neq x(1)\).
  • \(e_{-2}=2\neq 1=d_{-2}^{(2)}\implies y\neq x(2)\).
  • \(e_{-3}=1\neq 4=d_{-3}^{(3)}\implies y\neq x(3)\).
  • And so on.

4. Given \(n\in\mathbb{N}\)

\[ e_{-n}\neq d_{-n}^{(n)}\implies y\neq x(n). \]

But \(e_{-n}\neq d_{-n}^{(n)}\) by definition.

Therefore the distinguished decimal representation

\[ 0.e_{-1}e_{-2}e_{-3}\ldots \]

lies outside the collection of distinguished decimal representations

\[ \{0.d_{-1}^{(n)}d_{-2}^{(n)}\ldots:n\in\mathbb{N}\} \]

obtainable from \(x:\mathbb{N}\to(0,1]\).

Therefore there cannot exist a bijection \(x:\mathbb{N}\to(0,1]\) and so \((0,1]\) is uncountable.

Corollary

\(\mathbb{R}\) is uncountable.

Proof.

Since \((0,1]\subset\mathbb{R}\) is uncountable, \(\mathbb{R}\) is uncountable.

├── the-absolute-value-and-triangle-inequality/
├── the-absolute-value/
Absolute Value

For \(x\in\mathbb{R}\), define

\[ |x|= \begin{cases} x, & x\geq0,\\ -x, & x<0. \end{cases} \]

Absolute value has the following properties:

  1. \(|x|\geq0\), and \(|x|=0\) if and only if \(x=0\).
  2. \(|-x|=|x|\).
  3. \(|xy|=|x||y|\).
  4. \(|x^2|=x^2=|x|^2\).
  5. \(|x|\leq y\) if and only if \(-y\leq x\leq y\).
  6. \(x\leq|x|\).

Exercise.

Prove each of these properties directly from the definition of absolute value.

└── the-triangle-inequality/
Theorem: triangle inequality

For all \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ |x+y|\leq|x|+|y|. \]

Proof.

1. Observe

\[ |x+y|\leq|x|+|y| \iff -(|x|+|y|)\leq x+y\leq |x|+|y|. \]

2. But

\[ x\leq|x|,\ y\leq|y|\implies x+y\leq|x|+|y|. \]

3. and

\[ -x\leq|-x|,\ -y\leq|-y|\implies -x-y\leq|-x|+|-y|\implies-(|x|+|y|)\leq x+y. \]

4. Combining 2. and 3. gives

\[ -(|x|+|y|)\leq x+y\leq|x|+|y|. \]
├── sequences-and-limits/
├── outline/

So we've formalized the real numbers. Now what?

We begin studying real analysis proper.

In this unit we:

  • Define sequences of real numbers.
  • Formalize notions of convergence and divergence.
  • Study properties and examples of sequences.
├── why-sequences/

There are at least two situations in which it is natural to consider sequences and their convergence.

  1. A sequence may encode a mathematical object; e.g., a Fourier series:

    \[ \{c_n\} \iff f\sim\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_ne^{inx}. \]

    A natural question is: What properties of \(f\) are characterized by type of convergence of the sequence \(\{c_n\}\)?

  1. When solving certain problems, you may try generating a sequence of approximate solutions hoping the sequence converges to a solution.

    For example, suppose we wish to calculate

    \[ x=\operatorname*{argmin}\Psi \]

    of some function \(\Psi\). One might try constructing or showing existence of \(\{x_n\}\) satisfying

    \[ \Psi(x_n)\geq\Psi(x_{n+1})\geq\cdots, \qquad \Psi(x_n)\to\min\Psi, \]

    hoping \(\{x_n\}\) converges to some \(x\).

├── sequences-of-real-numbers/
Sequences

A sequence of real numbers is a function

\[ x:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}. \]

Notation:

  • Often write \(x_n\) for \(x(n)\) and understand \(x_n\) as the \(n\)th term in the sequence.
  • Often write
\[ \{x_n\}_{n=1}^{\infty},\quad \{x_n\},\quad \text{ or }\quad x_1,x_2,\ldots \]

for a sequence \(x:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}\).

Bounded Sequences

A sequence \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded if there exists \(M\geq0\) such that

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n|\leq M. \]

Example.

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be the sequence defined by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=\frac{1}{n}. \]

Given \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ n\geq1\implies \frac{1}{n}\leq1. \]

Thus

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n|=\frac{1}{n}\leq1. \]

Therefore \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded with \(M=1\).

Sequences versus Sets

Important: a sequence is a function \(x:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}\), not a set of real numbers.

For example

\[ \{(-1)^n\}_{n=1}^{\infty} \]

is the function \(x:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}\) defined by \(x(n)=(-1)^n\), whereas

\[ \{(-1)^{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}=\{-1,1\}. \]
├── convergence-of-sequences/
Convergence

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence and \(x\in\mathbb{R}\).

We say \(\{x_n\}\) converges to \(x\) if:

\(\forall\,\epsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ n\geq N\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon. \]

In this case we write

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty} x_n = x\quad \text{or}\quad \lim x_n = x\quad \text{or}\quad x_n\to x \]

and say \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent and has limit \(x\).

If \(\{x_n\}\) is not convergent, we say it is divergent.

Illustration

Which \(N\) seems to satisfy \(n\geq N\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon\)?

Note: \(|x_n-x|<\epsilon\iff x-\epsilon<x_n<x+\epsilon\).

Divergence

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence.

To show that \(\{x_n\}\) does not converge to \(x\in\mathbb{R}\), one must show:

There exists \(\epsilon>0\) such that for all \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) there exists \(n\geq N\) such that

\[ |x_n-x|\geq\epsilon. \]

Viz., \(\{x_n\}\) always admits a term a positive distance away from \(x\).

In particular: \(\{x_n\}\) is divergent if this holds for every \(x\in\mathbb{R}\).

Illustration

Observe below that every odd term remains more than \(\epsilon\) away from 1.

├── uniqueness-of-limits/

Goal: Prove

  • two real numbers arbitrarily close to each other must be the same number;
  • if a sequence converges, then its limit is unique.

Note. There are "geometric" settings where limits of sequences are not unique.

Theorem

Let \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\) be such that

\[ |x-y|<\epsilon \]

for all \(\epsilon>0\).

Then \(x=y\).

Proof.

For contradiction, suppose \(x\neq y\).

Need to use

\[ \forall\,\epsilon>0,\quad |x-y|<\epsilon \]

to establish contradiction.

Idea: choose specific \(\epsilon>0\).

Observe: \(x\neq y\) gives

\[ |x-y|>0 \]

Idea: choose \(\epsilon=|x-y|\).

But then

\[ (\forall\,\epsilon>0,\ |x-y|<\epsilon)\implies |x-y|<|x-y|, \]

a contradiction.

Theorem: Uniqueness of Limits

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence in \(\mathbb{R}\) and let \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\).

If \(x_n\to x\) and \(x_n\to y\), then \(x=y\).

Proof.

Plan: show that

\[ \forall \epsilon>0,\quad |x-y|<\epsilon. \]

The preceding theorem would then imply \(x=y\).

We have

(i) \(x_n\to x\)

(ii) \(x_n\to y\)

and want

(iii) \(|x-y|<\epsilon\) for all \(\epsilon>0\).

Idea: combine (i) and (ii) to obtain (iii).

Observe

(i) \(x_n\to x\) \(\iff \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists N_1\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(n\geq N_1\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon\)

(ii) \(x_n\to y\) \(\iff \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists N_2\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(n\geq N_2\implies |x_n-y|<\epsilon\)

Recall: triangle inequality asserts

\[ \forall a,b,c\in\mathbb{R},\quad |a-b|\leq|a-c|+|b-c|. \]

Idea: use (i) and (ii) with triangle inequality to obtain the estimate

\[ \forall\epsilon>0,\quad |x-y|<\epsilon. \]

Let \(\epsilon>0\) be arbitrary.

Let \(N_1,N_2\in\mathbb{N}\) be such that

(i) \(n\geq N_1\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon/2\)

(ii) \(n\geq N_2\implies |x_n-y|<\epsilon/2\)

Let \(N=\max\{N_1,N_2\}\).

Then \(n\geq N \implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon/2\) and \(|x_n-y|<\epsilon/2\).

Combining with the triangle inequality, for all \(n\geq N\) there holds

\[ |x-y|=|x-x_n+x_n-y|\leq|x-x_n|+|x_n-y|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon. \]

Since \(\epsilon>0\) was arbitrary, we conclude \(x=y\).

└── examples/
Example

(i) Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and define \(\{x_n\}\) by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=c. \]

Then

\[ \lim x_n=c. \]

(ii) Define \(\{x_n\}\) by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=3+\frac{(-1)^n}{n}. \]

Then

\[ \lim x_n=3. \]

Proof.

(i) Let \({x_n}\) be defined by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=c. \]

Let \(\epsilon>0\) and choose \(N=1\).

For every \(n\geq N\),

\[ |x_n-c|=|c-c|=0<\epsilon. \]

Therefore \(\lim x_n=c\).

(ii) Let \({x_n}\) be defined by

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad x_n=3+\frac{(-1)^n}{n}. \]

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

By the Archimedean property, choose \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ N>\frac1\epsilon. \]

Then for every \(n\geq N\),

\[ |x_n-3|=\left|\frac{(-1)^n}{n}\right|=\frac1n\leq\frac1N<\epsilon. \]

Therefore \(\lim x_n=3\).

├── convergent-sequences-are-bounded/
├── goals/
  1. Partially characterize convergent sequences; i.e., offer an answer to "What do all convergent sequences necessarily satisfy?"
  2. Witness the proof strategy of splitting a problem into special cases.
└── theorem-and-proof/
Theorem

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of real numbers.

If \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent, then \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded: \(\exists M\geq0\) such that

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n|\leq M. \]

Proof.

Suppose \(x_n\to x\); i.e., \(\forall\epsilon>0\), \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ n\geq N\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon. \]

Observe: Given \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), if \(C>0\) and if

\[ |x_n-x|<C, \]

then

\[ |x_n| =|x_n-x+x| \leq|x-x_n|+|x| \leq C+|x|. \]

Idea: If \(\epsilon>0\) and if \(|x_n-x|<\epsilon\) for \(n\geq N\), then we need only control finitely many terms

\[ |x_1|,|x_2|,\ldots,|x_{N-1}|. \]
  • Fix \(\epsilon>0\) and let \(N\in\mathbb{N}\), \(N\geq2\), be such that
\[ n\geq N\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon. \]
  • Define
\[ M'=\max\{|x_1|,|x_2|,\ldots,|x_{N-1}|\}. \]
  • Then
\[ |x_n|\leq M' \qquad\text{for }n=1,\ldots,N-1, \]

and

\[ |x_n| \leq|x-x_n|+|x| \leq\epsilon+|x| \qquad\text{for }n\geq N. \]
  • Lastly, define
\[ M=\max\{M',\epsilon+|x|\}. \]
  • Then
\[ |x_n|\leq \begin{cases} M', & \text{if }n=1,\ldots,N-1,\\ \epsilon+|x|, & \text{if }n\geq N \end{cases} \leq M. \]
  • Since \(|x_n|\leq M\) for all \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), we conclude \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded.

In short:

├── monotone-sequences/
├── goals/
  1. Identify a sufficient condition for sequence convergence.
  2. In particular, we identify a class of sequences whose convergent sequences are completely characterized by a necessary and sufficient condition.
├── definition-and-examples/
Monotone Sequences

A sequence \(\{x_n\}\) is monotone increasing if, for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ x_n\leq x_{n+1}. \]

A sequence \(\{x_n\}\) is monotone decreasing if, for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ x_n\geq x_{n+1}. \]

If \(\{x_n\}\) is either monotone increasing or monotone decreasing, we say that \(\{x_n\}\) is monotone.

Examples
  • \(a_n=\frac1n\) is monotone decreasing.
  • \(b_n=-\frac1n\) is monotone increasing.
  • \(c_n=2(-1)^n\) is neither monotone increasing nor monotone decreasing.
└── monotone-convergence/
Monotone Increasing Sequences

Remark. The preceding theorem asserts: if \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent, then \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded.

In general, the converse is false; e.g., consider

\[ x_n=(-1)^n. \]

However, if \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded and monotone, then \(\{x_n\}\) indeed converges.

This result is contained in the following two theorems.

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a monotone increasing sequence.

Then \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent if and only if \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded above.

Moreover,

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}x_n = \sup\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}. \]

Proof.

1. We have from the preceding theorem that, if \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent, then \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded and hence bounded above.

Suppose now \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded above and monotone increasing.

2. Since \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded above, by the least upper bound property,

\[ x:=\sup\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\} \]

exists.

We prove

\[ x_n\to x. \]

Viz., \(\forall\epsilon>0\), \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ n\geq N\implies |x-x_n|<\epsilon. \]

3. Let \(\epsilon>0\) be arbitrary.

Since

\[ x=\sup\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}, \]

there exists \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ x-\epsilon<x_N\leq x. \]

Recall: the existence of such an \(x_N\) is guaranteed by a characterization of supremum we proved before.

4. Since

\[ x-\epsilon<x_N\leq x,\qquad x=\sup\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}, \]

and \(\{x_n\}\) is monotone increasing, it follows

\[ x-\epsilon<x_N\leq x_n\leq x \]

for all \(n\geq N\).

5. Since

\[ \forall n\geq N,\qquad x-\epsilon<x_N\leq x_n\leq x, \qquad x=\sup\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}, \]

there holds

\[ 0\leq x-x_n<\epsilon, \]

and therefore

\[ \forall n\geq N,\qquad |x-x_n|=x-x_n<\epsilon. \]

6. We have therefore shown: \(\forall\epsilon>0\), \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ n\geq N\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon; \]

i.e.,

\[ \lim x_n = x = \sup\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}. \]
Monotone Decreasing Sequences

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a monotone decreasing sequence.

Then \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent if and only if \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded below.

Moreover,

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}x_n = \inf\{x_n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}. \]

Proof.

The proof is similar to the monotone increasing case, replacing the supremum by the infimum and reversing the inequalities.

├── subsequences/
├── motivation/

Imagine an "almost monotonic" sequence such as

\[ x_n= \begin{cases} 1/n & \text{if \(n\) is even}\\ 1/n^3 & \text{if \(n\) is odd} \end{cases} \qquad\text{or}\qquad y_n= \begin{cases} 1+1/n & \text{if \(n\) is even}\\ 1/n & \text{if \(n\) is odd.} \end{cases} \]

Evidently \(\{x_n\}\) ought to still converge and \(\{y_n\}\) ought to diverge.

This ought to be true because all terms in \(\{x_n\}\) behave similarly, whereas, \(\{y_n\}\) admits two "subsequences" converging to the distinct real numbers \(0\) and \(1\).

An Aside. Suppose \(\{y_n\}\) above represents a minimizing sequence of some function \(\Psi\):

\[ \Psi(y_n)\geq\Psi(y_{n+1}), \qquad \Psi(y_n)\to\min\Psi. \]

This suggests \(\Psi\) may have two minimizing inputs: \(0\) and \(1\).

The point: a divergent sequence may still admit useful subsequences.

├── definition-and-examples/
Subsequences

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence and let \(\{n_k\}\) be a strictly increasing sequence of natural numbers.

Then the sequence

\[ \{x_{n_k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty} \]

is called a subsequence of \(\{x_n\}\).

Example 1.

If \(\{x_n\}\) is any sequence, then \(\{x_{2n}\}\) and \(\{x_{2n-1}\}\) define the subsequences of even and odd terms of \(\{x_n\}\), respectively.

Example 2. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be defined by \(x_n = (-1)^n\).

Then

\[ \{x_{2n}\} = \{1,1,1,1,1,\ldots\} \] \[ \{x_{2n-1}\} = \{-1,-1,-1,-1,-1,\ldots\} \]

define two distinct convergent subsequences with different limits.

├── limits-of-subsequences/
Theorem: Limits of Subsequences

If \(x_n\to x\), then every subsequence \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) satisfies

\[ x_{n_k}\to x. \]

Observe. Since \(\{n_k\}\) is a strictly increasing sequence of natural numbers,

\[ n_k\geq k \qquad\text{for every }k\in\mathbb{N}. \]

Proof.

Suppose \(x_n\to x\). Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Choose \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ n\geq M\implies |x_n-x|<\epsilon. \]

If \(k\geq M\), then

\[ n_k\geq k\geq M, \]

and therefore

\[ |x_{n_k}-x|<\epsilon. \]

Hence \(x_{n_k}\to x\).

└── using-subsequences-to-prove-divergence/
Using Subsequences to Prove Divergence

Let \(\{x_n\}\) be defined by

\[ x_n=(-1)^n. \]

Its odd and even subsequences satisfy

\[ x_{2k-1}=-1 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad x_{2k-1}\to-1, \] \[ x_{2k}=1 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad x_{2k}\to1. \]

Thus \(\{x_n\}\) has two subsequences converging to distinct limits.

If \(\{x_n\}\) converged to some \(x\in\mathbb{R}\), then every subsequence would converge to \(x\).

But

\[ x_{2k-1}\to-1 \qquad\text{and}\qquad x_{2k}\to1. \]

Therefore \(\{(-1)^n\}\) diverges.

├── limit-theorems-for-sequences/
├── squeeze-theorem/
The Squeeze Theorem

Theorem. Let \(\{a_n\}\), \(\{b_n\}\), and \(\{x_n\}\) be sequences of real numbers, and let \(x\in\mathbb{R}\).

Suppose that for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ a_n\leq x_n\leq b_n. \]

Suppose also that \(\{a_n\}\) and \(\{b_n\}\) converge to the same limit:

\[ \lim a_n=x=\lim b_n. \]

Then \(\{x_n\}\) converges and

\[ \lim x_n=x. \]
Proof

WTS.

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad x-\varepsilon<x_n<x+\varepsilon \]

Idea. Show both

\[ x-\varepsilon<a_n\ \text{and}\ b_n<x+\varepsilon \]

then appeal to \(a_n\leq x_n\leq b_n\).

Let \(\varepsilon>0\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \textbf{(i)}\quad a_n\to x &\implies \exists N_1\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N_1,\ |a_n-x|<\varepsilon \\ &\implies \forall n\geq N_1,\ x-\varepsilon<a_n. \end{aligned} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \textbf{(ii)}\quad b_n\to x &\implies \exists N_2\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N_2,\ |b_n-x|<\varepsilon \\ &\implies \forall n\geq N_2,\ b_n<x+\varepsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Choose

\[ N=\max\{N_1,N_2\}. \]

Combining

\[ \forall n\geq N_1,\quad x-\varepsilon<a_n \] \[ \forall n\geq N_2,\quad b_n<x+\varepsilon \] \[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad a_n\leq x_n\leq b_n \]

we obtain

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad x-\varepsilon<a_n\leq x_n\leq b_n<x+\varepsilon \implies \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-x|<\varepsilon. \]

Since \(\varepsilon>0\) was arbitrary, we conclude \(x_n\to x\).

├── equivalent-definition-of-convergence/
An Equivalent Way to Check Convergence

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of real numbers and let \(x\in\mathbb{R}\).

Then

\[ \lim x_n=x \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \lim |x_n-x|=0. \]

Proof.

Observe:

\[ x_n\to x \]

\(\Longleftrightarrow\)

\[ \forall \varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N} \text{ s.t. } \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-x|<\varepsilon \]

\(\Longleftrightarrow\)

\[ \forall \varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N} \text{ s.t. } \forall n\geq N,\quad \bigl||x_n-x|-0\bigr|=|x_n-x|<\varepsilon \]

\(\Longleftrightarrow\)

\[ |x_n-x|\to0. \]

\(\square\)

├── example/
Example

Show that

\[ \lim \frac{n^2+1}{n^2+n}=1. \]

By the equivalent definition of convergence, this means showing that

\[ \left|\frac{n^2+1}{n^2+n}-1\right|\to0. \]

Observe

\[ \begin{aligned} \left|\frac{n^2+1}{n^2+n}-1\right| &=\left|\frac{1-n}{n^2+n}\right|\\ &=\frac{n-1}{n(n+1)}\\ &\leq\frac{n+1}{n(n+1)}\\ &=\frac{1}{n}. \end{aligned} \]

Thus

\[ 0\leq \left|\frac{n^2+1}{n^2+n}-1\right| \leq\frac1n\to0. \]

By the squeeze theorem,

\[ \left|\frac{n^2+1}{n^2+n}-1\right|\to0. \]

By the equivalent definition of convergence,

\[ \lim \frac{n^2+1}{n^2+n}=1. \]
├── limits-and-order/
How Limits Interact with Ordering

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) and \(\{y_n\}\) be convergent sequences of real numbers, and let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\).

(i) If \(x_n\leq y_n\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), then

\[ \lim x_n\leq \lim y_n. \]

(ii) If \(a\leq x_n\leq b\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), then

\[ a\leq \lim x_n\leq b. \]

Remark. In both statements, “for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\)” can be relaxed to “for every sufficiently large \(n\in\mathbb{N}\)”.

Proof of (i): \(x_n\leq y_n\implies \lim x_n\leq\lim y_n\)

Let

\[ x=\lim x_n, \qquad y=\lim y_n. \]

Suppose for contradiction that \(y<x\); we show \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(y_N<x_N\), contradicting \(\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ x_n\leq y_n\).

Observe: for sufficiently large \(n\),

\[ x-\varepsilon<x_n\quad \text{and} \quad y_n<y+\varepsilon \]

Idea. Choose \(\varepsilon>0\) so that

\[ y+\varepsilon\leq x-\varepsilon \]

Letting

\[ \varepsilon:=\frac{x-y}{2}>0, \]

observe

\[ \begin{aligned} x_n\to x &\implies \exists N_0\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N_0,\ |x_n-x|<\varepsilon\\ &\implies \forall n\geq N_0,\ x_n>x-\varepsilon=\frac{x+y}{2}, \end{aligned} \]

and

\[ \begin{aligned} y_n\to y &\implies \exists N_1\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N_1,\ |y_n-y|<\varepsilon\\ &\implies \forall n\geq N_1,\ y_n<y+\varepsilon=\frac{x+y}{2}. \end{aligned} \]

Choose

\[ N=\max\{N_0,N_1\}. \]

Then, by work above,

\[ y_N<\frac{x+y}{2}<x_N, \]

contradicting \(\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ x_n\leq y_n\), thereby proving (i).

Proof of (ii): \(a\leq x_n\leq b\implies a\leq\lim x_n\leq b\)

The constant sequences \(\{a\}\) and \(\{b\}\) converge to \(a\) and \(b\), respectively.

Since \(a\leq x_n\leq b\), applying (i) twice gives

\[ a=\lim a \leq\lim x_n \leq\lim b=b. \]

This proves (ii).

├── algebra-of-limits/
Algebra of Limits

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) and \(\{y_n\}\) be sequences of real numbers, and let \(x,y\in\mathbb{R}\).

Suppose

\[ \lim x_n=x, \qquad \lim y_n=y. \]

Then:

  1. \(x_n+y_n\to x+y\).
  2. For every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\), \(cx_n\to cx\).
  3. \(x_ny_n\to xy\).
  4. If \(y_n\neq0\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(y\neq0\), then \(x_n/y_n\to x/y\).
1. Limits Are Additive: \(x_n+y_n\to x+y\)

WTS:

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n+y_n-(x+y)|<\varepsilon. \]

Triangle inequality gives

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n+y_n-(x+y)|\leq |x_n-x|+|y_n-y|. \]

Idea: use \(x_n\to x\) and \(y_n\to y\).

Remark. Note we cannot yet appeal to the squeeze theorem here.

\[ \textbf{(i)}\quad x_n\to x\implies \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N_1\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N_1,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \] \[ \textbf{(ii)}\quad y_n\to y\implies \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N_2\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N_2,\quad |y_n-y|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \]

Letting \(N=\max\{N_1,N_2\}\):

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \quad\text{and}\quad |y_n-y|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \]

Combining

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n+y_n-(x+y)|\leq |x_n-x|+|y_n-y| \]

and

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \quad\text{and}\quad |y_n-y|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}, \]

we conclude

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n+y_n-(x+y)|<\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(x_n+y_n\to x+y\).

2. Limits Under Scalar Multiplication: \(cx_n\to cx\)

Note: if \(c=0\), then \(cx_n=0\) and \(cx_n\to0\) trivially.

Assume \(c\neq0\).

WTS:

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |cx_n-cx|<\varepsilon \]

Observe:

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |cx_n-cx|=|c|\,|x_n-x|. \]

Idea: use \(x_n\to x\) with suitably chosen “\(\varepsilon\)” to control \(|c|\,|x_n-x|\).

Using \(x_n\to x\),

\[ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{|c|} \]

Combining with

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |cx_n-cx|=|c|\,|x_n-x|. \]

we conclude

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |cx_n-cx|=|c|\,|x_n-x|<\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(cx_n\to cx\).

3. Limits of Products: \(x_ny_n\to xy\)

WTS:

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_ny_n-xy|<\varepsilon. \]

Observe:

\[ \begin{aligned} \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_ny_n-xy| &=|x_ny_n-y_nx+y_nx-xy|\\ &\leq |y_n(x_n-x)|+|x(y_n-y)|\\ &=|y_n|\,|x_n-x|+|x|\,|y_n-y|. \end{aligned} \]

Idea. control \(|y_n|\), \(|x_n-x|\), and \(|y_n-y|\); e.g., try showing

\[ \exists M\geq0\ \text{and}\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.} \] \[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |y_n|\leq M,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(M+1)},\quad |y_n-y|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(|x|+1)}. \]

Let \(\varepsilon>0\) be arbitrary.

(i) Using \(y_n\to y\), \(\exists N_1\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ \forall n\geq N_1,\quad |y_n-y|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(|x|+1)} \]

(ii) Using \(y_n\to y\), \(\{y_n\}\) is bounded and hence \(\exists M\geq0\) such that

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |y_n|\leq M. \]

(iii) Using \(x_n\to x\), \(\exists N_2\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ \forall n\geq N_2,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(M+1)} \]

Let \(N=\max\{N_1,N_2\}\).

Combining

  • \[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |y_n|\,|x_n-x|\leq M|x_n-x| \]
  • \[ \forall n\geq N_1,\quad |y_n-y|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(|x|+1)} \]
  • \[ \forall n\geq N_2,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2(M+1)} \]
  • \[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_ny_n-xy|\leq |y_n|\,|x_n-x|+|x|\,|y_n-y| \]

we conclude

\[ \begin{aligned} \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_ny_n-xy| &\leq M\frac{\varepsilon}{2(M+1)}+|x|\frac{\varepsilon}{2(|x|+1)} \\ &<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Therefore \(x_ny_n\to xy\).

4. Limits of Quotients: \(\frac{x_n}{y_n}\to\frac{x}{y}\)

By 3., it suffices to show

\[ \frac1{y_n}\to\frac1y. \]

Proof. Proof by being a problem in pset3.

├── square-roots-and-absolute-values/
Square Roots

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a convergent sequence of real numbers such that \(x_n\geq0\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).

Then

\[ \lim \sqrt{x_n} = \sqrt{\lim x_n}. \]

Proof. Let

\[ x=\lim x_n. \]

Since \(x_n\geq0\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), the order theorem gives \(x\geq0\).

We show \(\sqrt{x_n}\to\sqrt{x}\) by considering the cases \(x=0\) and \(x>0\).

Case 1: \(x=0\)

WTS:

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |\sqrt{x_n}-0|<\varepsilon. \]

Observe:

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |\sqrt{x_n}-0|=\sqrt{x_n}. \]

Idea. Use observation that \(\sqrt{x_n}<\varepsilon\) iff \(x_n<\varepsilon^2\).

Let \(\varepsilon>0\) be arbitrary.

Using \(x_n\to0\), \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n|=x_n<\varepsilon^2. \]

Combining with the observation above,

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |\sqrt{x_n}-0|=\sqrt{x_n}<\sqrt{\varepsilon^2}=\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(\sqrt{x_n}\to0=\sqrt{x}\).

Case 2: \(x>0\)

WTS:

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |\sqrt{x_n}-\sqrt{x}|<\varepsilon. \]

Observe:

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |\sqrt{x_n}-\sqrt{x}| =\frac{|x_n-x|}{\sqrt{x_n}+\sqrt{x}} \leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}|x_n-x|. \]

Idea. Since \(\sqrt{x}\) does not depend on \(n\), control \(|x_n-x|\) in terms of “\(\varepsilon\)” depending on \(\sqrt{x}\).

Let \(\varepsilon>0\) be arbitrary.

Since \(x>0\), we have \(\varepsilon\sqrt{x}>0\).

Using \(x_n\to x\), \(\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-x|<\varepsilon\sqrt{x}. \]

Combining with the observation above,

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |\sqrt{x_n}-\sqrt{x}| \leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}|x_n-x| <\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(\sqrt{x_n}\to\sqrt{x}\).

Absolute Values

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of real numbers and let \(x\in\mathbb{R}\).

If \(x_n\to x\), then

\[ |x_n|\to |x|. \]
Proof

First note that for every real number \(t\),

\[ \sqrt{t^2}=|t|. \]

Since \(x_n^2\to x^2\), the square root theorem gives

\[ \lim |x_n| =\lim \sqrt{x_n^2} =\sqrt{x^2} =|x|. \]
├── binomial-theorem/

Definition. For \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), define \(n\) factorial by

\[ n!:=1\cdot2\cdots n,\qquad 0!:=1. \]

For integers \(0\leq k\leq n\), the binomial coefficient is

\[ \binom nk:=\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}. \]

Observe.

\[ \binom n0=\binom nn=1. \]

Theorem (Binomial Theorem). For \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ (a+b)^n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk a^{n-k}b^k. \]

Proof. Follows from inducting on the exponent \(n\) and Pascal's identity:

\[ \binom nk+\binom n{k-1}=\binom{n+1}{k}\qquad(1\leq k\leq n). \]

The details are left as an exercise.

Corollary (binomial inequality). For \(t\geq0\) and \(n\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ (1+t)^n\geq1+nt,\qquad (1+t)^n\geq\binom nk t^k\quad(0\leq k\leq n). \]

Proof. Take \(a=1\) and \(b=t\):

\[ (1+t)^n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk t^k. \]

Every term is nonnegative, and the \(k=0,1\) terms sum to \(1+nt\).

├── geometric-sequences/
Geometric Sequences

Theorem. Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

If \(0<c<1\), then

\[ \lim c^n=0. \]

If \(c>1\), then \(\{c^n\}\) is unbounded.

Proof.

The Case \(0<c<1\)

Outline.

  1. Show \(\{c^n\}\) is bounded below and monotone decreasing, and hence has limit \(L\).
  2. Show \(\forall\varepsilon>0,\ |L|<\varepsilon\), and hence \(L=0\).

1. We show by induction

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad 0<c^{n+1}<c^n<1. \]

Base case: \(n=1\).

\[ 0<c<1 \implies 0<c^2<c<1 \]

by multiplication by \(c\).

Inductive step.

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad \bigl(0<c^{n+1}<c^n<1\ \text{ and }\ 0<c<1\bigr) \implies 0<c^{n+2}<c^{n+1}<1. \]

Consequently, \(\{c^n\}\) is bounded below and monotone decreasing.

2. Since 1. \(\implies\) \(\{c^n\}\) is bounded and monotone, \(\exists L\in\mathbb{R}\) s.t.

\[ L=\lim c^n. \]

We show \(L=0\) by showing \(\forall\varepsilon>0,\ |L|<\varepsilon\).

WTS. \(\forall\varepsilon>0,\ |L|<\varepsilon\).

This suggests using \(c^n\to L\) to have access to “\(\forall\varepsilon>0\)”.

Observe, for every \(N\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ \begin{aligned} |L| &=\frac{1-c}{1-c}|L| = \frac{1}{1-c}|L-cL|\\ &\leq \frac{1}{1-c}\left(|L-c^{N+1}|+|c^{N+1}-cL|\right)\\ &=\frac{1}{1-c}\left(|L-c^{N+1}|+c|L-c^N|\right). \end{aligned} \]

Note. We now have access to using \(c^n\to L\).

Since \(c^n\to L\):

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad |L-c^n|<\varepsilon \]

Then

\[ |L|\leq\frac{1}{1-c}\left(|L-c^{N+1}|+c|L-c^N|\right) <\frac{1+c}{1-c}\varepsilon. \]

Since \(\frac{1+c}{1-c}>0\), this implies \(\forall\varepsilon>0,\ |L|<\varepsilon\) and hence \(L=0\).

In conclusion: if \(0<c<1\), then

\[ \lim c^n=0. \]
The Case \(c>1\)

WTS. \(c>1\implies\{c^n\}\text{ is unbounded}\); i.e.,

\[ \forall B\geq0,\ \exists n\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ c^n>B. \]

Let \(B\geq0\) be arbitrary.

Use the Archimedean property and \(c>1\) to choose \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ n>\frac{B}{c-1}. \]

Using the binomial inequality and \(c>1\):

\[ \begin{aligned} c^n &=(1+(c-1))^n\\ &\geq 1+n(c-1)\\ &\geq n(c-1)\\ &>B. \end{aligned} \]

Since \(B\) was arbitrary, \(\{c^n\}\) is unbounded.

├── negative-powers/

Theorem. For each fixed \(p>0\),

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-p}=0. \]

WTS.

\[ \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq N,\quad \left|\frac{1}{n^p}-0\right|=\left|\frac{1}{n^p}\right|<\epsilon. \]

Observe. Since \(p>0\),

\[ \frac{1}{n^p}<\epsilon \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad n>\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/p}. \]

Proof. Let \(\epsilon>0\) be arbitrary.

By Archimedean property:

\[ \exists N\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ N>\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)^{1/p}. \]

Consequently

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad\frac{1}{n^p}\leq\frac{1}{N^p}<\epsilon. \]

Since \(\epsilon\) was arbitrary, it follows that \(1/n^p\to0\).

├── roots-of-a-fixed-positive-number/

Theorem. For each fixed \(p>0\),

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}p^{1/n}=1. \]

Here \(p^{1/n}\) denotes the positive \(n\)th root of \(p\).

Proof outline. First handle \(p>1\).

Then handle \(0<p<1\) by taking reciprocals and \(p=1\) directly.

The Case \(p>1\)

WTS. \(p^{1/n}-1\to0\).

Observe. Set \(u_n=p^{1/n}-1>0\).

By the binomial inequality,

\[ p=(1+u_n)^n\geq1+nu_n. \]

Idea. Since \(p\) is fixed and \(n\to\infty\), intuitively \(u_n\) must tend to \(0\).

This also suggests trying the squeeze theorem.

Observe

\[ p\geq1+nu_n\;\Rightarrow\;0\leq u_n\leq\frac{p-1}{n}. \]

Moreover

\[ \frac1n\to0\;\Rightarrow\;\frac{p-1}{n}\to0. \]

Therefore the squeeze theorem implies

\[ u_n\to0 \]

and so

\[ p^{1/n}=1+u_n\to1. \]
The Case \(0<p<1\)

Idea. The number \(q=1/p\) satisfies \(q>1\), so the preceding case applies.

\[ \begin{gathered} q^{1/n}\to1,\\ p^{1/n}=\frac{1}{q^{1/n}}\to\frac11=1. \end{gathered} \]

Recall: if \(\{x_n\}\) is a sequence with \(x_n>0\) and \(x_n\to L>0\), then

\[ \frac{1}{x_n}\to\frac{1}{L}. \]
The Case \(p=1\)

Observe. If \(p=1\), then \(p^{1/n}=1\) for every \(n\), and so \(p^{1/n}\to1\).

Conclusion

We have therefore shown that \(p^{1/n}\to1\) for all \(p>0\).

└── roots-of-n/

Theorem.

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}n^{1/n}=1. \]

Observe. \(n^{1/n}\to1\) if and only if \(n^{1/n}-1\to0\); however, neither \(n^{1/n}\) nor \(n^{1/n}-1\) are particularly easy to work with.

Idea. Let us consider instead \(u_n=n^{1/n}-1\geq0\) and write \(n=(1+u_n)^n\).

Note. This gives us access to the binomial theorem:

\[ n=(1+u_n)^n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk u_n^k. \]

We will use this to squeeze \(u_n\) between \(0\) and a negative power of \(n\) and hence show \(u_n\to0\).

More precisely: given

\[ n=(1+u_n)^n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk u_n^k \qquad\text{and}\qquad \binom nk=\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}, \]

we may try to obtain an estimate of the form

\[ n\geq c n^{a}u_n^{b} \]

with \(a,b>1\) and \(c>0\).

Indeed, then

\[ 0\leq u_n\leq\left(\frac{n^{1-a}}{c}\right)^{1/b}\to0. \]

Observe.

Given the binomial theorem

\[ n=(1+u_n)^n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk u_n^k,\qquad \binom nk=\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}, \]

we have, for \(n\geq2\),

\[ n\geq\binom n1 u_n=nu_n,\qquad n\geq\binom n2 u_n^2=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}u_n^2,\qquad\ldots \]

Idea. Using \(k=2\), we would obtain

\[ n\geq\frac{n(n-1)}{2}u_n^2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0\leq u_n\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{n-1}}. \]

Proof. Setting \(u_n=n^{1/n}-1\geq0\), we show \(u_n\to0\).

The binomial theorem implies, for \(n\geq2\),

\[ n=(1+u_n)^n\geq\frac{n(n-1)}{2}u_n^2 \]

and hence

\[ 0\leq u_n\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{n-1}}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt n}\to0\qquad(n\geq2). \]

By the squeeze theorem, \(u_n\to0\) and hence \(n^{1/n}\to1\).

├── limsup-and-liminf/
├── tails-of-a-sequence/

Definition. Given a sequence \(\{x_k\}\) and \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), its \(n\)th tail \(T_n\) is defined as

\[ T_n=\{x_k:k\geq n\}. \]

Observe.

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad T_{n+1}\subset T_n. \]
\[ x_1,\;x_2,\;\underbrace{x_3,\;x_4,\;\underbrace{x_5,\;x_6,\;x_7,\;\ldots}_{T_5}}_{T_3} \]

Proposition. Let \(\{x_k\}\) be a bounded real sequence.

Set

\[ a_n=\sup T_n,\qquad b_n=\inf T_n. \]
