Then \(\{a_n\}\) is decreasing and bounded, and \(\{b_n\}\) is increasing and bounded.

Proof. Recall

  • \(T_n=\{x_k:k\geq n\}\), \(a_n=\sup T_n\) and \(b_n=\inf T_n\);
  • if \(A\subset B\) are nonempty bounded sets, then \(\inf B\leq\inf A\leq\sup A\leq\sup B\);
  • \(\{x_k\}\text{ bounded}\implies\exists B>0\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall k\in\mathbb{N},\ |x_k|\leq B\).

Consequently,

\[ a_{n+1}=\sup T_{n+1}\leq\sup T_n=a_n,\qquad b_n=\inf T_n\leq\inf T_{n+1}=b_{n+1} \]

and

\[ -B\leq b_n\leq x_n\leq a_n\leq B. \]

Therefore \(\{a_n\},\{b_n\}\) are monotonic and bounded.

├── liminf-and-limsup/

Definition. Let \(\{x_k\}\) be a bounded sequence of real numbers.

Given \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), let

\[ a_n=\sup T_n=\sup\{x_k:k\geq n\},\quad b_n=\inf T_n=\inf\{x_k:k\geq n\}. \]

Then the limit inferior and limit superior of \(\{x_k\}\) are defined as

\[ \liminf_{n\to\infty}x_n=\lim_{n\to\infty}b_n\quad\text{and}\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}x_n=\lim_{n\to\infty}a_n, \]

respectively.

Remark.

  • \(b_n\leq a_n\implies\liminf x_n\leq\limsup x_n\).
  • \(\{x_n\}\text{ bounded}\implies\liminf x_n\text{ and }\limsup x_n\text{ exist}\).
├── examples-of-limsup-and-liminf/

Example. Let \(x_n=(-1)^n\).

Every tail has

\[ T_n=\{-1,1\},\qquad b_n=\inf T_n=-1,\qquad a_n=\sup T_n=1. \]

Therefore

\[ \liminf_{n\to\infty}(-1)^n=-1,\qquad \limsup_{n\to\infty}(-1)^n=1. \]

Example. Let \(x_n=1/n\).

Then

\[ T_n=\left\{\frac1k:k\geq n\right\},\qquad a_n=\sup T_n=\frac1n,\qquad b_n=\inf T_n=0. \]

Consequently

\[ \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n=\lim_{n\to\infty}0=0\quad\text{and}\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n=0. \]
├── subsequences-approaching-limsup-and-liminf/

Theorem. Every bounded real sequence \(\{x_n\}\) has subsequences satisfying

\[ x_{n_k}\to\limsup x_n,\qquad x_{m_k}\to\liminf x_n. \]

Observe. For \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(a_N=\sup T_N\), the \(\epsilon\)-characterization of suprema gives

\[ \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists j_\epsilon\geq N\ \text{s.t.}\ a_N-\epsilon<x_{j_\epsilon}\leq a_N. \]

Idea. Take \(\epsilon=1/k\) and choose terms from later and later tails, each within \(1/k\) of its tail supremum.

As the tail suprema approach \(\limsup x_n\), these chosen terms should too.

Proof (limsup). Set \(L=\limsup x_n\).

We construct the subsequence \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) by using the observation to choose a strictly increasing sequence of indices \(\{n_k\}\).

For \(k=1\), take \(N=1\) and \(\epsilon=1\).

The observation gives an index \(n_1=j_1\geq1\) satisfying

\[ a_1-1<x_{n_1}\leq a_1. \]

For \(k\geq2\), suppose \(n_1<\cdots<n_{k-1}\) have been chosen.

Take \(N=n_{k-1}+1\) and \(\epsilon=1/k\).

Apply the observation to this new tail \(T_N\) and choose \(n_k=j_{1/k}\geq N\) satisfying

\[ a_N-\frac1k<x_{n_k}\leq a_N. \]

This ensures \(n_k>n_{k-1}\), so \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) is a subsequence.

Since the indices strictly increase, \(n_k\geq k\), and hence \(n_{k-1}+1\to\infty\).

Consequently,

\[ a_{n_{k-1}+1}\to L,\qquad \frac1k\to0. \]

By construction, for \(k\geq2\),

\[ a_{n_{k-1}+1}-\frac1k<x_{n_k}\leq a_{n_{k-1}+1}. \]

The squeeze theorem gives \(x_{n_k}\to L=\limsup x_n\).

Exercise. Prove the liminf case.

Use the infimum property:

\[ \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists j_\epsilon\geq N\ \text{s.t.}\ b_N\leq x_{j_\epsilon}<b_N+\epsilon. \]
├── bolzano-weierstrass/

Theorem (Bolzano–Weierstrass). Every bounded real sequence has a convergent subsequence.

Proof. The preceding theorem gives a subsequence converging to \(\limsup x_n\in\mathbb{R}\).

└── when-limsup-and-liminf-agree/

Theorem. A bounded real sequence \(\{x_n\}\) converges if and only if

\[ \liminf x_n=\limsup x_n. \]

In this case,

\[ \lim x_n=\liminf x_n=\limsup x_n. \]

Proof. If \(\liminf x_n=\limsup x_n=L\), then

\[ b_n\leq x_n\leq a_n,\qquad b_n\to L,\qquad a_n\to L. \]

By squeeze, \(x_n\to L\).

Conversely, if \(x_n\to L\), every subsequence converges to \(L\).

Applying this to the subsequences approaching \(\liminf x_n\) and \(\limsup x_n\), uniqueness of limits gives

\[ \liminf x_n=L=\limsup x_n. \]
├── cauchy-sequences/
├── definition/
Cauchy Sequences

Definition. A sequence of real numbers \(\{x_n\}\) is Cauchy if

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n,k\geq M,\quad |x_n-x_k|<\varepsilon. \]
\[ { \def\CauchyPlace#1#2#3{\rlap{\kern#1em\raise#2em\hbox{$#3$}}} \CauchyPlace{5.6}{0}{\color{#dce5ec}\rule{10em}{3em}} \CauchyPlace{8.4}{0}{\color{#cedfd4}\rule{7.2em}{1.5em}} \CauchyPlace{8.4}{0}{\color{#365c50}\rule{0.035em}{1.5em}} \CauchyPlace{1.4}{0}{\rule{14.5em}{0.035em}} \CauchyPlace{1.4}{0}{\rule{0.035em}{6.5em}} \CauchyPlace{1.15}{6.35}{\scriptstyle\blacktriangle} \CauchyPlace{8.4}{-0.14}{\rule{0.035em}{0.14em}} \CauchyPlace{9.8}{-0.14}{\rule{0.035em}{0.14em}} \CauchyPlace{11.2}{-0.14}{\rule{0.035em}{0.14em}} \CauchyPlace{12.6}{-0.14}{\rule{0.035em}{0.14em}} \CauchyPlace{16.1}{1.2}{\color{#365c50}\varepsilon_2=\tfrac14} \phantom{\rule[-2.15em]{21em}{9.9em}} } \]

Remark. We will see that Cauchy \(\Rightarrow\) convergence in \(\mathbb{R}\).

Thus, the Cauchy condition provides another means to check convergence.

This matches intuition: if members of tails of a sequence become arbitrarily close, the sequence ought to converge.

However, in general spaces, Cauchy sequences need not converge; e.g., \(\mathbb{Q}\) or \(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\).

├── a-cauchy-example/

Example. Show that \(x_n=1/n\) is Cauchy.

WTS.

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n,k\geq M,\quad \left|\frac1n-\frac1k\right|<\varepsilon. \]

Observe. For \(n,k\geq M\),

\[ \left|\frac1n-\frac1k\right|\leq\frac1n+\frac1k\leq\frac2M. \]

Idea. Choose \(M\) so that \(1/M<\varepsilon/2\).

Proof. Let \(\varepsilon>0\).

By the Archimedean property, choose \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) with \(M>2/\varepsilon\).

Then, for all \(n,k\geq M\),

\[ \left|\frac1n-\frac1k\right| \leq\frac1n+\frac1k\leq\frac2M<\varepsilon. \]

Since \(\varepsilon>0\) was arbitrary, \(\{1/n\}\) is Cauchy.

├── a-non-cauchy-example/

Negation. A sequence \(\{x_n\}\) is not Cauchy if

\[ \exists\varepsilon_0>0\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall M\in\mathbb{N},\ \exists n,k\geq M,\, |x_n-x_k|\geq\varepsilon_0. \]

Remark. Thus, a sequence is not Cauchy if there is a uniform threshold \(\varepsilon_0>0\) such that every tail \(T_M=\{x_n:n\geq M\}\) contains two members at least \(\varepsilon_0\) apart.

Example. Show that \(x_n=(-1)^n\) is not Cauchy.

Observe. Consecutive terms satisfy

\[ |(-1)^M-(-1)^{M+1}|=2. \]

Idea. Choose \(\varepsilon_0=1\) and use consecutive terms beyond any proposed threshold.

Proof. Choose \(\varepsilon_0=1\) and let \(M\in\mathbb{N}\).

Take \(n=M\) and \(k=M+1\).

Then \(n,k\geq M\) and

\[ |x_n-x_k|=|(-1)^M-(-1)^{M+1}|=2\geq\varepsilon_0. \]

Thus \(\{(-1)^n\}\) is not Cauchy.

├── cauchy-sequences-are-bounded/

Theorem. Every Cauchy sequence of real numbers is bounded.

Remark. Note we are NOT assuming \(\{x_n\}\) is convergent.

Boundedness comes from \(\{x_n\}\) being Cauchy.

WTS.

\[ \exists B>0\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n|\leq B. \]

Observe. The Cauchy condition with \(\varepsilon=1\) gives \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n,k\geq M,\quad |x_n-x_k|<1. \]

Idea. The Cauchy condition offers control on the tail \(\{x_M,x_{M+1},\ldots\}\).

The set \(\{x_1,\ldots,x_{M-1}\}\) is bounded by being finite.

Proof. \(\{x_n\}\) being Cauchy \(\Rightarrow\exists M\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ \forall n,k\geq M,\quad |x_n-x_k|<1. \]

Triangle inequality and taking \(k=M\) give

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad |x_n|\leq|x_n-x_M|+|x_M|<1+|x_M|. \]

Set

\[ B=|x_1|+\cdots+|x_M|+1. \]

Then

\[ \begin{array}{@{}l@{\;}r@{\;}l@{}} \text{(finiteness)} & 1\!\leq\! n\!<\!M & \!\Rightarrow\! |x_n|\!<\!B \\[0.4em] \text{(Cauchy)} & n\!\geq\! M & \!\Rightarrow\! |x_n|\!<\!1\!+\!|x_M|\!\leq\! B. \end{array} \]

Consequently

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |x_n|\!\leq\! B \]

and so \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded.

├── subsequential-convergence-implies-convergence/

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a Cauchy sequence of real numbers.

If a subsequence \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) converges to \(x\), then \(x_n\to x\).

Remark. This theorem asserts that one may establish convergence by checking the Cauchy condition and finding any convergent subsequence.

WTS. \(x_{n_k}\to x\) and \(\{x_n\}\) Cauchy imply

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq M,\quad |x_n-x|<\varepsilon. \]

Observe. For any subsequence term \(x_{n_k}\),

\[ |x_n-x|\leq|x_n-x_{n_k}|+|x_{n_k}-x|. \]

Idea. Use the Cauchy condition for the first term and subsequence convergence for the second.

Proof. Let \(\varepsilon>0\).

(i) Since \(x_{n_k}\to x\), choose \(M_0\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall k\geq M_0,\quad |x_{n_k}-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \]

(ii) Since \(\{x_n\}\) is Cauchy, choose \(M_1\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n,m\geq M_1,\quad |x_n-x_m|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \]

Set \(M=M_0+M_1\), noting \(n_M\geq M\).

Observe that

\[ M\geq M_0\implies |x_{n_M}-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \] \[ n,n_M\!\geq\!M\!\Rightarrow\!n,n_M\!\geq\!M_1\!\Rightarrow\!|x_n\!-\!x_{n_M}|\!<\!\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \]

For every \(n\geq M\), the triangle inequality gives

\[ |x_n\!-\!x|\!\leq\!|x_n\!-\!x_{n_M}|\!+\!|x_{n_M}\!-\!x|\!<\!\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\!+\!\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\!=\!\varepsilon. \]

In conclusion: \(x_n\to x\).

└── cauchy-implies-convergence/

Theorem. A sequence of real numbers is Cauchy if and only if it converges.

Remark.

  1. This is an existence theorem: if \(\{x_n\}\) is Cauchy, then \(\exists x\in\mathbb{R}\) such that \(x_n\to x\).

    This does not require a priori identification of the limit \(x\).

  2. The implication “Cauchy \(\Rightarrow\) convergent” is called Cauchy completeness.

    In particular, \(\mathbb{R}\) is Cauchy complete, while \(\mathbb{Q}\) is not.

Proof \((\Longrightarrow)\). Suppose \(\{x_n\}\) is Cauchy.

  1. By the boundedness theorem, \(\{x_n\}\) is bounded.
  2. By Bolzano–Weierstrass, \(\{x_n\}\) has a convergent subsequence.
  3. By the preceding theorem, \(\{x_n\}\) converges.

Here, completeness in this form follows from Bolzano–Weierstrass, which ultimately rests on the least-upper-bound property.

Proof \((\Longleftarrow)\). Let \(\varepsilon>0\).

Since \(x_n\to x\), choose \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad |x_n-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}. \]

For every \(n,k\geq M\),

\[ |x_n-x_k|\leq|x_n-x|+|x_k-x|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}=\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(\{x_n\}\) is Cauchy.

├── series/
├── series-and-partial-sums/

Definition. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of real numbers.

For \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), its \(n\)th partial sum is

\[ s_n:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}x_k=x_1+\cdots+x_n. \]

The sequence \(\{s_n\}\) is called the sequence of partial sums.

Definition. If the partial sums \(\{s_n\}\) of a sequence \(\{x_n\}\) converge to \(s\), then define

\[ \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}x_k:=\lim_{n\to\infty}s_n=s \]

to be the series of \(\{x_n\}\) and say it converges to \(s\).

If \(\{s_n\}\) is not convergent, we say \(\{x_n\}\) has a divergent series.

Remark. A series need not start at \(k=1\).

Instead, one may define a series starting at any finite integer index or with lower limit \(-\infty\).

We will often write \(\sum x_n\) for \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\).

Optional Remark. For any set \(A\subset[0,\infty)\), define

\[ \sum_{a\in A}a:=\sup\left\{\sum_{a\in E}a:\ E\subseteq A,\ E\text{ finite}\right\}. \]

The empty sum is \(0\), and we write \(+\infty\) if the finite subsums are unbounded above.

This definition also makes sense in any ordered field, provided the supremum exists in that field.

The least upper bound property is not needed to state the definition.

It guarantees existence when the finite subsums are bounded above.

├── a-telescoping-series/

Example. Show that

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n(n+1)} \]

converges.

WTS. The partial sums

\[ s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}\frac{1}{n(n+1)} \]

converge.

Observe.

\[ \frac{1}{n(n+1)}=\frac1n-\frac1{n+1}. \]

Idea. Substitute this identity into the finite sum and cancel the repeated terms.

Proof. Consider the partial sums:

\[ \begin{aligned} s_m &=\sum_{n=1}^{m}\left(\frac1n-\frac1{n+1}\right)\\ &=\left(1+\frac12\!+\!\cdots\!+\!\frac1m\right)\\ &\qquad-\left(\frac12\!+\!\cdots\!+\!\frac1m+\frac1{m+1}\right)\\ &=1-\frac1{m+1}\longrightarrow1. \end{aligned} \]

Thus the partial sums converge, and

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n(n+1)}=1. \]
├── geometric-series/

Definition. For \(\alpha,r\in\mathbb{R}\), a series of the form

\[ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\alpha r^n \]

is called a geometric series.

Theorem. If \(r\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(|r|<1\), then the series converges and

\[ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n=\frac{1}{1-r}. \]

Observe. For \(m\geq0\),

\[ \begin{aligned} s_m&=\sum_{n=0}^{m}r^n=1+r+\cdots+r^m,\\ rs_m&=r+r^2+\cdots+r^{m+1}. \end{aligned} \]

For \(r\neq1\), subtracting gives

\[ (1-r)s_m=1-r^{m+1}\quad\implies\quad s_m=\frac{1-r^{m+1}}{1-r}. \]

This formula can also be proved by induction.

Idea. Now use \(|r|<1\) and the limit laws.

Proof. Since \(|r|<1\), we have \(1-r\neq0\) and

\[ |r^{m+1}|=|r|^{m+1}\longrightarrow0. \]

Thus \(r^{m+1}\to0\).

Using the finite-sum formula and the limit laws,

\[ s_m=\sum_{n=0}^{m}r^n=\frac{1-r^{m+1}}{1-r} \longrightarrow\frac{1-0}{1-r}=\frac{1}{1-r}. \]

Therefore the series converges and

\[ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n=\frac{1}{1-r}. \]
├── tails-of-a-series/

Theorem. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of real numbers and let \(M\in\mathbb{N}\).

Then

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\text{ converges} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sum_{n=M}^{\infty}x_n\text{ converges}. \]

Proof. For \(m\geq M\), we have \(s_m=t_m+C\), where

\[ s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_n,\qquad t_m=\sum_{n=M}^{m}x_n,\qquad C=\sum_{n=1}^{M-1}x_n. \]

When \(M=1\), the empty sum defining \(C\) is \(0\).

If \(s_m\to s\), then \(t_m=s_m-C\longrightarrow s-C\).

If \(t_m\to t\), then \(s_m=t_m+C\longrightarrow t+C\).

Thus either series converges if and only if the other does.

├── cauchy-series-and-the-cauchy-criterion/

Definition. A series \(\sum x_n\) is Cauchy if its sequence of partial sums \(\{s_m\}\) is Cauchy:

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall m,\ell\geq M,\quad \left|\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_n-\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}x_n\right|<\varepsilon. \]

Theorem. A series of real numbers is Cauchy if and only if it converges.

Remark. One usually calls the condition

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall\ell>m\geq M,\quad \left|\sum_{n=m+1}^{\ell}x_n\right|<\varepsilon \]

the Cauchy criterion for series.

Proof. Apply the Cauchy criterion for real sequences to \(\{s_m\}\):

\[ \begin{aligned} \sum x_n\text{ is Cauchy} &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \{s_m\}\text{ is Cauchy}\\ &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \{s_m\}\text{ converges}\\ &\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \sum x_n\text{ converges}. \end{aligned} \]
├── convergence-implies-terms-vanish/

Theorem. If \(\sum x_n\) converges, then

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}x_n=0. \]

WTS.

\[ \forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall n\geq M,\quad |x_n|<\varepsilon. \]

Observe. The Cauchy criterion controls \(\left|\sum_{j=m+1}^{\ell}x_j\right|\).

Idea. Take \(m=n-1\) and \(\ell=n\) so that this sum has just one term:

\[ \sum_{j=(n-1)+1}^{n}x_j=x_n. \]

Proof. Suppose \(\sum x_n\) converges and let \(\varepsilon>0\).

\(\text{Convergence}\implies\text{Cauchy}\implies\exists M_0\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t.

\[ \forall\ell>m\geq M_0,\quad \left|\sum_{j=m+1}^{\ell}x_j\right|<\varepsilon. \]

Set \(M=M_0+1\).

If \(n\geq M\), then \(n,n-1\geq M_0\) and so

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad |x_n|=\left|\sum_{j=n}^{n}x_j\right|<\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(x_n\to0\).

├── when-geometric-series-diverge/

Theorem. If \(r\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(|r|\geq1\), then

\[ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n \]

diverges.

Proof. If \(|r|\geq1\), then

\[ |r^n|=|r|^n\geq1\qquad\text{for every }n\geq0. \]

By the squeeze theorem, even if \(r^n\) converged, its limit could not be \(0\).

If \(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n\) converged, the preceding theorem would imply \(r^n\to0\), a contradiction.

Therefore the series diverges.

Corollary. Let \(r\in\mathbb{R}\).

Then

\[ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n\text{ converges} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad |r|<1. \]

Proof. Combine

\[ |r|<1\implies\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n\text{ converges} \]

and

\[ |r|\geq1\implies\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r^n\text{ diverges}. \]
└── harmonic-series/

Recall. If \(\sum x_n\) converges, then \(x_n\to0\).

Question. Is the converse true?

Does \(x_n\to0\) imply that \(\sum x_n\) converges?

Theorem. The series

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1n \]

does not converge.

Remark. This series is called the harmonic series.

Since \(1/n\to0\) and the harmonic series diverges, having terms that tend to zero does not imply convergence.

Observe. For \(m,\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}\) with \(m\leq\ell\):

  1. The sum \(\sum_{n=m}^{\ell}\) contains \(\ell-m+1\) terms.
  2. \(\displaystyle \sum_{n=m}^{\ell}\frac1n\geq\sum_{n=m}^{\ell}\frac1\ell=\frac{\ell-m+1}{\ell}.\)
  3. \(\displaystyle 2^k-2^{k-1}=2^{k-1}\) and \(\displaystyle \frac{2^{k-1}}{2^k}=\frac12.\)
  4. \(\displaystyle \sum_{n=1}^{2^\ell}x_n=x_1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}x_n.\)

Taking \(m=2^{k-1}+1\) and \(\ell=2^k\) gives

\[ \sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}\frac1n\geq\frac{2^{k-1}}{2^k}=\frac12. \]

Proof. Let \(s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}1/n\).

For every \(k\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ \sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}\frac1n \geq\sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}\frac1{2^k} =\frac{2^{k-1}}{2^k} =\frac12. \]

Consequently, for every \(\ell\in\mathbb{N}\):

\[ \begin{aligned} s_{2^\ell}&=\sum_{n=1}^{2^\ell}\frac1n =1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}\frac1n\\ &\geq1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\frac12=1+\frac{\ell}{2}, \end{aligned} \]

and so \(\{s_m\}\) is unbounded and therefore \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}1/n\) diverges.

Remark. The decomposition

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{2^\ell}\frac1n =1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}\frac1n \]

is an example of a dyadic decomposition and the proof above is a dyadic argument.

This is a powerful and standard type of argument in analysis.

├── properties-of-series/
├── linearity-of-series/

Theorem (Linearity of Series). Let \(\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\), and suppose that \(\sum x_n\) and \(\sum y_n\) converge.

Then \(\sum(\alpha x_n+y_n)\) converges and

\[ \sum(\alpha x_n+y_n) =\alpha\sum x_n+\sum y_n. \]

Proof. For every \(m\in\mathbb{N}\), finite sums satisfy

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{m}(\alpha x_n+y_n) =\alpha\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_n+\sum_{n=1}^{m}y_n. \]

Taking limits and using the algebra of limits gives

\[ \lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{n=1}^{m}(\alpha x_n+y_n) =\alpha\sum x_n+\sum y_n. \]

Thus \(\sum(\alpha x_n+y_n)\) converges to the stated sum.

├── series-with-nonnegative-terms/

Theorem. Suppose \(x_n\geq0\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).

Then

\[ \sum x_n\text{ converges} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \{s_m\}\text{ is bounded}, \]

where \(s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_n\).

Proof \((\Longrightarrow)\). Suppose \(\sum x_n\) converges.

Then the sequence of partial sums \(\{s_m\}\) converges and hence is bounded.

Proof \((\Longleftarrow)\). Suppose \(\{s_m\}\) is bounded.

Since

\[ s_m\leq s_m+x_{m+1}=s_{m+1} \]

we conclude \(\{s_m\}\) is monotonic and bounded, and hence converges by the monotone convergence theorem.

└── absolute-convergence/

Definition. The series \(\sum x_n\) converges absolutely if

\[ \sum |x_n| \]

converges.

Theorem. If \(\sum x_n\) converges absolutely, then \(\sum x_n\) converges.

Proof. Suppose \(\sum |x_n|\) converges and let \(\varepsilon>0\).

By the Cauchy criterion,

\[ \exists M\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall\ell>m\geq M,\quad \sum_{n=m+1}^{\ell}|x_n|<\varepsilon. \]

Iterating the triangle inequality gives

\[ \forall\ell>m\geq M,\quad \left|\sum_{n=m+1}^{\ell}x_n\right| \leq\sum_{n=m+1}^{\ell}|x_n|<\varepsilon. \]

Therefore \(\sum x_n\) is Cauchy and hence convergent.

Remark. Let \(x_n=(-1)^n/n\).

We will show \(\sum x_n\) converges, but it is not absolutely convergent since

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|x_n|=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac1n \]

is the harmonic series.

Optional Remark. Let \(\ell^1(\mathbb{N})\) denote the family of real sequences \(\{x_n\}\) such that \(\sum |x_n|\) converges.

The preceding results ensure that \(\ell^1(\mathbb{N})\) is a vector space and that we may define the map

\[ \begin{gathered} \sum:\ell^1(\mathbb{N})\to\mathbb{R},\\ \{x_n\}\mapsto\sum x_n:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n. \end{gathered} \]

The linearity theorem establishes that \(\sum\) is a linear functional on \(\ell^1(\mathbb{N})\).

Informally, \(\sum\) is a kind of “integral” defined on the naturals.

├── convergence-tests/
├── comparison-test/

Theorem (Comparison Test). Suppose

\[ 0\leq x_n\leq y_n\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}). \]

Then

  1. if \(\sum y_n\) converges, then \(\sum x_n\) converges;
  2. if \(\sum x_n\) diverges, then \(\sum y_n\) diverges.

Proof. Let

\[ s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_n \qquad\text{and}\qquad t_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}y_n. \]

Then \(s_m\leq t_m\), and both \(\{s_m\}\) and \(\{t_m\}\) are monotonically increasing.

1. Suppose \(\sum y_n\) converges.

Then \(\{t_m\}\) is bounded and so

\[ \exists B\geq0\ \text{s.t.}\ \forall m\in\mathbb{N},\quad 0\leq s_m\leq t_m\leq B. \]

But then \(\{s_m\}\) is monotonic and bounded.

Monotone convergence theorem \(\implies\{s_m\}\) converges.

2. Suppose \(\sum x_n\) diverges.

Then \(\{s_m\}\) is unbounded and so

\[ \forall B\geq0,\ \exists m\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\quad B\leq s_m\leq t_m. \]

But then \(\{t_m\}\) is unbounded and hence divergent.

├── p-series/

Theorem (\(p\)-Series). Let \(p\in\mathbb{R}\).

Then

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^p} \text{ converges} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad p>1. \]

Proof \((\Longrightarrow)\). Let \(p\in\mathbb{R}\) and suppose \(\sum 1/n^p\) converges.

For contradiction, assume \(p\leq1\).

Then

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad0\leq\frac1n\leq\frac{1}{n^p}. \]

By comparison with the harmonic series, \(\sum 1/n^p\) diverges.

This contradiction implies \(p>1\).

Proof \((\Longleftarrow)\). Suppose \(p>1\).

We perform a dyadic decomposition and compare with a geometric series.

Let \(s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}1/n^p\) and \(r=2^{1-p}\in(0,1)\).

For every \(\ell\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ \begin{aligned} s_{2^\ell}&=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sum_{n=2^{k-1}+1}^{2^k}\frac1{n^p}\\ &\leq1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\frac{2^{k-1}}{2^{p(k-1)}}\\ &=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}r^{k-1}\leq1+\frac1{1-r}. \end{aligned} \]

Since \(\{s_m\}\) is increasing and \(m<2^m\),

\[ \forall m\in\mathbb{N},\quad0\leq s_m\leq s_{2^m}\leq1+\frac1{1-r}. \]

Monotone convergence theorem \(\Longrightarrow\) \(\{s_m\}\) converges.

Hence \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}1/n^p\) converges.

├── ratio-test/

Theorem (Ratio Test). Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of nonzero real numbers, and suppose

\[ L=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{|x_{n+1}|}{|x_n|} \]

exists.

  1. If \(L<1\), then \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\) converges absolutely.
  2. If \(L>1\), then \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\) diverges.

Proof. Case \(L>1\).

Choose \(1<\alpha<L\) and \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad\frac{|x_{n+1}|}{|x_n|}>\alpha>1. \]

In particular

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad|x_{n+1}|>|x_n|\geq|x_M|>0. \]

Thus \(\{x_n\}\) does not converge to \(0\), so \(\sum x_n\) diverges.

Case \(L<1\).

Choose \(0\leq L<\alpha<1\) and \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad\frac{|x_{n+1}|}{|x_n|}<\alpha<1. \]

In particular

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad|x_{n+1}|<\alpha|x_n|. \]

Iterating, for \(n\geq M\),

\[ \begin{aligned} |x_{M+1}|&\leq\alpha|x_M|\\ |x_{M+2}|&\leq\alpha|x_{M+1}|\leq\alpha^2|x_M|\\ &\vdots\\ |x_n|&\leq\alpha^{n-M}|x_M|. \end{aligned} \]

For \(m\geq M\),

\[ \begin{aligned} \sum_{n=M}^{m}|x_n| &\leq|x_M|\sum_{n=M}^{m}\alpha^{n-M}\\ &=|x_M|\sum_{k=0}^{m-M}\alpha^k\\ &\leq\frac{|x_M|}{1-\alpha}. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(\sum_{n=M}^{\infty}|x_n|\) converges by the monotone convergence theorem.

This is enough to conclude absolute convergence of \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\).

├── example/

Example. For every \(x\in\mathbb{R}\), the series

\[ \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^n}{n!} \]

converges absolutely.

Proof. For \(x=0\), the series \(1+0+0+\cdots\) converges absolutely.

For \(x\neq0\),

\[ \frac{|x^{n+1}/(n+1)!|}{|x^n/n!|} =\frac{|x|}{n+1}\longrightarrow0. \]

The ratio test gives absolute convergence.

├── an-inconclusive-ratio-test/

If \(|x_{n+1}|/|x_n|\to1\), we cannot conclude whether \(\sum x_n\) converges absolutely.

Indeed, \(\sum1/n^2\) converges and \(\sum1/n\) diverges, yet

\[ \frac{n^2}{(n+1)^2}\longrightarrow1 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \frac{n}{n+1}\longrightarrow1. \]
├── root-test/

Theorem (Root Test). Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence of real numbers, and suppose

\[ L=\lim_{n\to\infty}|x_n|^{1/n} \]

exists.

  1. If \(L<1\), then \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\) converges absolutely.
  2. If \(L>1\), then \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\) diverges.

Remark. When \(L=1\), the test is inconclusive.

Proof. Case \(L>1\).

Choose \(1<\alpha<L\) and \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad|x_n|^{1/n}>\alpha>1. \]

In particular

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad|x_n|>\alpha^n\geq\alpha^M>0. \]

Thus \(\{x_n\}\) does not converge to \(0\), so \(\sum x_n\) diverges.

Case \(L<1\).

Choose \(0\leq L<\alpha<1\) and \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad|x_n|^{1/n}<\alpha<1. \]

In particular

\[ \forall n\geq M,\quad|x_n|<\alpha^n. \]

For \(m\geq M\),

\[ \sum_{n=M}^{m}|x_n|\leq\sum_{n=M}^{m}\alpha^n=\alpha^M\sum_{k=0}^{m-M}\alpha^k\leq\frac{\alpha^M}{1-\alpha}. \]

Thus \(\sum_{n=M}^{\infty}|x_n|\) converges by the monotone convergence theorem.

This is enough to conclude absolute convergence of \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\).

Remark. The theorem also holds with

\[ L=\limsup_{n\to\infty}|x_n|^{1/n}. \]

The ordinary limit need not exist.

1. If \(L<1\), choose \(L<\alpha<1\).

Then \(|x_n|^{1/n}<\alpha\) eventually, so the same estimate applies.

2. If \(L>1\), then \(|x_n|^{1/n}>1\), hence \(|x_n|>1\), for arbitrarily large \(n\).

Thus \(\{x_n\}\) does not converge to \(0\), so \(\sum x_n\) diverges.

├── alternating-series-test/

Theorem (Alternating-Series Test). Suppose \(x_{n+1}\leq x_n\) for every \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), and \(x_n\to0\).

Then

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^n x_n \]

converges.

Proof outline.

  1. Show that the sequence of even partial sums \(\{s_{2k}\}\) converges to some \(s\in\mathbb{R}\).
  2. Use \[ |s_{2k+1}-s|\leq|s_{2k}-s|+|x_{2k+1}| \] and \(x_m\to0\) to conclude \(s_{2k+1}\to s\).
  3. Use \(s_{2k}\to s\) and \(s_{2k+1}\to s\) to conclude \(s_m\to s\).

Proof. Let \(s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}(-1)^n x_n\).

Since \(\{x_n\}\) decreases to \(0\), \(x_n\geq0\).

Then

\[ s_{2k}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}(x_{2j}-x_{2j-1}), \]

and monotonicity gives

\[ s_{2k+2}-s_{2k}=x_{2k+2}-x_{2k+1}\leq0. \]

Thus \(\{s_{2k}\}\) is monotone decreasing.

Observing \(\forall k\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ s_{2k}=-x_1+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(x_{2j}-x_{2j+1})+x_{2k}, \]

and for \(1\leq j\leq k-1\),

\[ x_{2j}-x_{2j+1}\geq0 \quad\text{and}\quad x_{2k}\geq0, \]

there holds

\[ \forall k\in\mathbb{N},\quad s_{2k}\geq-x_1. \]

By monotone convergence, \(s_{2k}\to s\) for some \(s\in\mathbb{R}.\)

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Since \(s_{2k}\to s\) and \(x_n\to0\), choose \(M_0,M_1\in\mathbb{N}\) such that

\[ \begin{aligned} k\geq M_0&\implies |s_{2k}-s|<\frac{\epsilon}{2},\\ n\geq M_1&\implies |x_n|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}. \end{aligned} \]

Set \(M=\max\{2M_0+1,M_1\}\), and let \(m\geq M\).

If \(m=2k\), then \(k\geq M_0\), so

\[ |s_m-s|=|s_{2k}-s|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}<\epsilon. \]

If \(m=2k+1\), then \(k\geq M_0\), \(m\geq M_1\), and \(s_m=s_{2k}-x_m\), so

\[ |s_m-s|\leq|s_{2k}-s|+|x_m| <\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon. \]

Therefore \(s_m\to s\), so the series converges.

└── alternating-harmonic-series/

Corollary. The series

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^n}{n} \]

converges, but does not converge absolutely.

Proof. Since \(1/n\) decreases to \(0\), the alternating-series test gives convergence.

Absolute convergence fails because

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{(-1)^n}{n}\right| =\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n} \]

diverges.

├── rearrangements/
└── absolute-convergence-and-rearrangements/

Theorem. Suppose \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n\) converges absolutely, with sum \(x\).

If \(\sigma:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\) is a bijection, then

\[ \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_{\sigma(n)} \]

converges absolutely and has sum \(x\).

The series \(\sum x_{\sigma(n)}\) is called a rearrangement of \(\sum x_n\).

Proof outline. Let

\[ s_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_n, \qquad t_m=\sum_{n=1}^{m}x_{\sigma(n)}. \]

  1. Prove the rearrangement converges absolutely.

    Idea. Every term in \(t_m\) occurs once in \(s_M\) for sufficiently large \(M\); compare the sums of absolute values.

  2. Prove \(s_m\) and \(t_m\) converge to the same sum \(x\).

    Idea. For fixed large \(K\), eventually \(t_m=s_K+r_m,\) where \(|r_m|\) is bounded by a finite tail of the Cauchy series \(\sum|x_n|\).

Proof (Absolute Convergence).

\(\sum |x_n|\) converges \(\implies\exists B\geq0\) s.t.

\[ \forall\ell\in\mathbb{N},\quad\sum_{n=1}^{\ell}|x_n|\leq B. \]

Let \(m\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(\ell=\max\{\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(m)\}\).

Observing \(\{\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(m)\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,\ell\}\), we have

\[ 0\leq\sum_{n=1}^{m}|x_{\sigma(n)}| \leq\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}|x_j|\leq B. \]

Monotone convergence theorem \(\implies\sum x_{\sigma(n)}\) is absolutely convergent.

Proof (Sums Agree).

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Observe

  • \(s_k\to x\implies\exists M_0\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\) \[ \forall k\geq M_0,\quad |s_k-x|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}, \]
  • \(\sum |x_n|\text{ Cauchy}\implies\exists M_1\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{s.t.}\) \[ \forall\ell>k\geq M_1,\quad\sum_{j=k+1}^{\ell}|x_j|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}. \]

Set \(K=\max\{M_0,M_1\}\).

Set

\[ \begin{gathered} M=\max\{\sigma^{-1}(1),\ldots,\sigma^{-1}(K)\},\\ E_m=\{\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(m)\},\quad m\geq M. \end{gathered} \]

Then

\[ \{1,\ldots,K\}\subseteq E_m \quad\text{and}\quad t_m=s_K+\sum_{j\in E_m,\,j>K}x_j. \]

Note.

(i) \(K=\max\{M_0,M_1\}\): \(M_0\) came from convergence and \(M_1\) from the Cauchy criterion.

(ii) Since \(K\geq M_0\), there holds \(|s_K-x|<\epsilon/2\).

(iii) If \(\ell=\max(\{K+1\}\cup E_m)\), then \(\ell>K\geq M_1\) and so

\[ \sum_{j\in E_m,\,j>K}|x_j| \leq\sum_{j=K+1}^{\ell}|x_j|<\frac{\epsilon}{2}. \]

Writing

\[ t_m-x=s_K-x+\sum_{j\in E_m,\,j>K}x_j, \]

(ii) controls \(s_K-x\), and (iii) controls the sum.

Altogether: for \(m\geq M\) and \(\ell=\max(\{K+1\}\cup E_m)\), there holds

\[ \begin{aligned} |t_m-x| &\leq|s_K-x|+\sum_{j\in E_m,\,j>K}|x_j|\\ &\leq|s_K-x|+\sum_{j=K+1}^{\ell}|x_j|\\ &<\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Hence \(t_m\to x\), i.e., \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_{\sigma(n)}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_n=x\).

├── limits-of-functions/
├── cluster-points/

Definition. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\).

A point \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) is a cluster point of \(S\) if

\[ \forall\delta>0,\quad (c-\delta,c+\delta)\cap(S\setminus\{c\})\neq\varnothing. \]

Equivalently,

\[ \forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in S\quad\text{s.t.}\quad 0<|x-c|<\delta. \]
\[ { \def\ClusterPlace#1#2#3{\rlap{\kern#1em\raise#2em\hbox{$#3$}}} \def\ClusterDot#1{\ClusterPlace{#1}{-0.125}{\kern0.05em\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}} {\color{#737373} \ClusterPlace{0.396}{0}{\rule{17.512em}{0.025em}} \ClusterPlace{0.176}{-0.175}{\scriptstyle\blacktriangleleft} \ClusterPlace{17.644}{-0.175}{\scriptstyle\blacktriangleright} } {\color{#3f6286} \ClusterPlace{1.188}{1.1}{S} \ClusterDot{0.594} \ClusterDot{1.122} \ClusterDot{1.958} \ClusterDot{3.454} \ClusterDot{3.7884} \ClusterDot{4.334} \ClusterDot{5.566} \ClusterDot{6.3404} \ClusterDot{6.666} \ClusterDot{7.634} \ClusterDot{8.05} \ClusterDot{8.35} \ClusterDot{8.58} \ClusterDot{8.76} \ClusterDot{8.89} \ClusterDot{8.98} \ClusterDot{9.215} \ClusterDot{9.325} \ClusterDot{9.485} \ClusterDot{9.695} \ClusterDot{9.955} \ClusterDot{10.275} \ClusterDot{10.714} \ClusterDot{11.7436} \ClusterDot{12.518} \ClusterDot{12.87} \ClusterDot{14.146} \ClusterDot{14.4716} \ClusterDot{15.158} \ClusterDot{16.302} \ClusterDot{16.918} } \ClusterPlace{1.95}{-0.25}{\underset{c-\delta_1}{\Biggl(}} \ClusterPlace{4.35}{-0.25}{\underset{c-\delta_2}{\Bigl(}} \ClusterPlace{6.55}{-0.25}{\underset{c-\delta_3}{\bigl(}} \ClusterPlace{10.55}{-0.25}{\underset{c+\delta_3}{\bigr)}} \ClusterPlace{12.75}{-0.25}{\underset{c+\delta_2}{\Bigr)}} \ClusterPlace{15.15}{-0.25}{\underset{c+\delta_1}{\Biggr)}} \ClusterPlace{9.25}{-0.17}{\rule{0.035em}{0.34em}} \ClusterPlace{9.06}{-1.15}{c} \ClusterPlace{18.304}{-0.25}{\mathbb{R}} \phantom{\rule[-2.3em]{19.2em}{4.4em}} } \]

Remark. A cluster point does not have to belong to the set.

Examples.

  1. \(S=\{1/n:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\): \(0\) is a cluster point.
  2. \(S=(0,1)\): the set of cluster points is \([0,1]\).
  3. \(S=\mathbb{Q}\): the set of cluster points is \(\mathbb{R}\).
  4. \(S=\{0\}\): there are no cluster points.
  5. \(S=\mathbb{Z}\): there are no cluster points.

Negation. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

The point \(c\) is not a cluster point of \(S\) if and only if

\[ \exists\delta_0>0\quad\text{s.t.}\quad (c-\delta_0,c+\delta_0)\cap(S\setminus\{c\})=\varnothing. \]

Equivalently,

\[ \exists\delta_0>0,\ \forall x\in S\setminus\{c\},\quad |x-c|\geq\delta_0. \]
├── cluster-points-and-sequences/

Theorem. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

Then \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\) if and only if there exists a sequence \(\{x_n\}\) in \(S\setminus\{c\}\) such that

\[ x_n\to c. \]

Proof \((\Rightarrow)\). Suppose \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\).

Given \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), let \(\delta_n=1/n\).

\[ \begin{aligned} &c\text{ is a cluster point of }S\\ &\implies\left(c-\frac1n,c+\frac1n\right)\cap(S\setminus\{c\})\neq\varnothing\\ &\implies\exists x_n\in\left(c-\frac1n,c+\frac1n\right)\cap(S\setminus\{c\})\\ &\implies\exists x_n\in S\quad\text{s.t.}\quad0<|x_n-c|<\frac1n. \end{aligned} \]

Selecting such \(x_n\), squeeze theorem implies \(x_n\to c\).

Proof \((\Leftarrow)\). Suppose \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\setminus\{c\}\) with \(x_n\to c\).

Let \(\delta>0\) and let \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) be s.t.

\[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-c|<\delta. \]

In particular \(x_N\neq c\) and

\[ x_N\in(c-\delta,c+\delta)\cap(S\setminus\{c\}) \]

and so

\[ \forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in(c-\delta,c+\delta)\cap(S\setminus\{c\}). \]

This means \(c\) is a cluster point.

├── defining-limit-of-a-function/

Definition. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\) be a cluster point of \(S\), and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

We say \(f(x)\) converges to \(L\in\mathbb{R}\) if:

\(\forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ 0<|x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \]

Notation. We write

\[ f(x)\to L\ \text{as }x\to c, \qquad\text{or}\qquad \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L. \]
\(L+\epsilon\) \(L\) \(L-\epsilon\) \(c-\delta\) \(c\) \(c+\delta\) \(y\) \(x\) \(y=f(x)\)

Remark. When calculating \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)\), we do not assume \(c\) is in the domain of \(f\).

Negation. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\) be a cluster point of \(S\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(L\in\mathbb{R}\).

\(f(x)\) does not converge to \(L\) as \(x\to c\) if and only if

\[ \begin{gathered} \exists\epsilon_0>0,\ \forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in S\quad\text{s.t.}\\ 0<|x-c|<\delta\quad\text{and}\quad |f(x)-L|\geq\epsilon_0. \end{gathered} \]
├── uniqueness-of-function-limits/

Theorem (Uniqueness of Function Limits). Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\) be a cluster point of \(S\), and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

If

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L_1 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L_2, \]

then \(L_1=L_2\).

Proof. We show \(|L_1-L_2|<\epsilon\) for every \(\epsilon>0\).

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Choose \(\delta_1,\delta_2>0\) such that, for \(x\in S\),

\[ \begin{aligned} 0<|x-c|<\delta_1&\implies |f(x)-L_1|<\frac\epsilon2,\\ 0<|x-c|<\delta_2&\implies |f(x)-L_2|<\frac\epsilon2. \end{aligned} \]

Set \(\delta=\min\{\delta_1,\delta_2\}>0\).

Since \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\), choose \(x_0\in S\) with

\[ 0<|x_0-c|<\delta. \]

Then

\[ \begin{aligned} |L_1-L_2| &=|L_1-f(x_0)+f(x_0)-L_2|\\ &\leq |L_1-f(x_0)|+|f(x_0)-L_2|\\ &<\frac\epsilon2+\frac\epsilon2=\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Since \(\epsilon>0\) was arbitrary, \(L_1=L_2\).

├── an-affine-function/

Example. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(f(x)=ax+b\).

For every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=ac+b. \]

Proof Sketch.

Observe

\[ \begin{aligned} |f(x)-(ac+b)|&=|ax+b-(ac+b)|\\ &=|a|\,|x-c|. \end{aligned} \]

Thus, if \(|x-c|<\delta\), then

\[ |f(x)-(ac+b)|\leq |a|\delta. \]

This suggests: let \(\delta>0\) be such that

\[ |a|\delta<\epsilon. \]

E.g., if \(\delta<\epsilon/(1+|a|)\), then

\[ |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-(ac+b)|<\epsilon. \]

Proof. Let \(\epsilon>0\) and set \(\delta=\epsilon/(1+|a|)>0\).

If \(x\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(0<|x-c|<\delta\), then

\[ \begin{aligned} |f(x)-(ac+b)| &=|a|\,|x-c|\\ &\leq |a|\delta\\ &=\frac{|a|}{1+|a|}\epsilon<\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=ac+b\).

├── the-square-root-function/

Theorem. Let \(f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\), \(f(x)=\sqrt{x}\).

For every \(c\geq0\),

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=\sqrt{c}. \]

Proof Sketch (\(c>0\)).

Observe, for \(x\geq0\),

\[ |\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{c}|=\frac{|x-c|}{\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{c}} \leq\frac{|x-c|}{\sqrt{c}}. \]

Thus, if \(|x-c|<\delta\), then

\[ |\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{c}|<\frac\delta{\sqrt{c}}. \]

This suggests: let \(\delta>0\) be such that

\[ \delta/\sqrt{c}<\epsilon. \]

Proof. Consider \(c>0\) first.

Let \(\epsilon>0\) and set \(\delta=\epsilon\sqrt{c}>0\).

If \(x\geq0\) and \(0<|x-c|<\delta\), then

\[ \begin{aligned} |\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{c}| &=\frac{|x-c|}{\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{c}}\\ &\leq\frac{|x-c|}{\sqrt{c}}\\ &<\frac\delta{\sqrt{c}}=\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

For \(c=0\), take \(\delta=\epsilon^2\).

├── sequential-characterization-of-limits/

Theorem. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\) be a cluster point of \(S\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(L\in\mathbb{R}\).

The following are equivalent:

  1. \(\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L\).
  2. For every sequence \(\{x_n\}\) in \(S\setminus\{c\}\) such that \(x_n\to c\), we have \(f(x_n)\to L\).

Proof \((1\Rightarrow2)\).

Suppose \(f(x)\to L\) as \(x\to c\).

Let \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\setminus\{c\}\) with \(x_n\to c\).

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Observe

  • \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L\implies\exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\), \[ 0<|x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \]
  • \(x_n\to c\implies\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t. \[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-c|<\delta. \]

Fix such a \(\delta>0\) and \(N>0\).

Altogether, if \(n\geq N\), then

\[ \begin{gathered} 0<|x_n-c|<\delta\\ (\text{by }x_n\to c\text{ and }x_n\neq c) \end{gathered} \]

and so

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall n\geq N,\quad |f(x_n)-L|<\epsilon\\ (\text{by }f(x)\to L\text{ as }x\to c). \end{gathered} \]

Consequently, \(f(x_n)\to L\).

Proof \((2\Rightarrow1)\).

Suppose: for every sequence \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\setminus\{c\}\), if \(x_n\to c\), then \(f(x_n)\to L\).

Assume for contradiction \(f(x)\) does not converge to \(L\) as \(x\to c\).

Then \(\exists\epsilon_0>0\) s.t. \(\forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in S\) s.t.

\[ 0<|x-c|<\delta \quad\text{and}\quad |f(x)-L|\geq\epsilon_0. \]

Fix such an \(\epsilon_0\).

Since \(f(x)\) does not converge to \(L\) as \(x\to c\),

\(\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ \exists x_n\in S\) s.t.

\[ 0<|x_n-c|<\frac1n \quad\text{and}\quad |f(x_n)-L|\geq\epsilon_0. \]

Fix such a sequence \(\{x_n\}\).

Altogether, \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\) and

\[ \begin{gathered} \bigl(\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad 0<|x_n-c|<1/n\bigr)\\ \implies x_n\to c\quad\text{and}\quad\{x_n\}\subseteq S\setminus\{c\}, \end{gathered} \]

and

\[ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |f(x_n)-L|\geq\epsilon_0>0. \]

However, by assumption \(x_n\to c\) implies \(f(x_n)\to L\), thereby establishing the contradiction

\[ 0=\lim_{n\to\infty}|f(x_n)-L|\geq\epsilon_0>0. \]
├── the-square-function/

Theorem. For every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}x^2=c^2. \]

Proof. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence in \(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{c\}\) with \(x_n\to c\).

The product rule for sequence limits gives

\[ x_n^2=x_n\,x_n\to c\,c=c^2. \]

The sequential characterization therefore gives

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}x^2=c^2. \]
├── oscillating-functions/

Theorem. Consider the following functions on \(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\).

  1. \(\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x\to0}\sin(1/x)\) does not exist.
  2. \(\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x\to0}x\sin(1/x)=0\).

Proof of 1. Set

\[ x_n=\frac{2}{(2n-1)\pi}\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}). \]

Then \(x_n\neq0\) and \(x_n\to0\), but

\[ \sin(1/x_n)=\sin\left(\frac{(2n-1)\pi}{2}\right)=(-1)^{n+1}. \]

The sequence \(\{(-1)^{n+1}\}\) does not converge.

By the sequential characterization, \(\lim\limits_{x\to0}\sin(1/x)\) does not exist.

Proof of 2. Let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence in \(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\) with \(x_n\to0\).

Since \(|\sin t|\leq1\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ \begin{aligned} 0\leq |x_n\sin(1/x_n)| &=|x_n|\,|\sin(1/x_n)|\\ &\leq |x_n|\to0. \end{aligned} \]

The squeeze theorem gives \(|x_n\sin(1/x_n)|\to0\), hence \(x_n\sin(1/x_n)\to0\).

The sequential characterization gives

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to0}x\sin(1/x)=0. \]
├── order-and-function-limits/

Theorem (Preservation of Order). Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\) be a cluster point of \(S\), and let \(f,g:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

Suppose \(f(x)\leq g(x)\) for every \(x\in S\), and both function limits at \(c\) exist.

Then

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)\leq\lim\limits_{x\to c}g(x). \]

Proof. Write \(L_1=\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)\) and \(L_2=\lim\limits_{x\to c}g(x)\).

Since \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\), choose a sequence \(\{x_n\}\) in \(S\setminus\{c\}\) with \(x_n\to c\).

By the sequential characterization and preservation of order for sequence limits,

\[ L_1=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(x_n) \leq\lim_{n\to\infty}g(x_n)=L_2. \]
├── one-sided-limits/

Definition (Left-Hand Limit). Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and suppose \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\cap(-\infty,c)\).

We say \(f(x)\) converges to \(L\in\mathbb{R}\) as \(x\to c^-\) if

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists\delta>0\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\forall x\in S,\\ c-\delta<x<c\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \end{gathered} \]

Notation. We write \(\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x\to c^-}f(x)=L\).

Definition (Right-Hand Limit). Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and suppose \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\cap(c,\infty)\).

We say \(f(x)\) converges to \(L\in\mathbb{R}\) as \(x\to c^+\) if

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists\delta>0\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\forall x\in S,\\ c<x<c+\delta\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \end{gathered} \]

Notation. We write \(\displaystyle\lim\limits_{x\to c^+}f(x)=L\).

├── a-step-function/

Example. Define \(f:\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}\) by

\[ f(x)=\begin{cases} 1,&x>0,\\ 0,&x<0. \end{cases} \]

Then

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to0^-}f(x)=0 \qquad\text{and}\qquad \lim\limits_{x\to0^+}f(x)=1. \]

Both one-sided limits exist even though \(f(0)\) is undefined.

└── two-sided-and-one-sided-limits/

Theorem. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(L\in\mathbb{R}\).

Suppose \(c\) is a cluster point of both

\[ S\cap(-\infty,c)\qquad\text{and}\qquad S\cap(c,\infty). \]

Then \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\), and \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L\) if and only if

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c^-}f(x)=\lim\limits_{x\to c^+}f(x)=L. \]

Proof. Let \(A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), and suppose \(c\) is a cluster point of \(A\).

Let \(\delta>0\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \emptyset&\neq(c-\delta,c+\delta)\cap(A\setminus\{c\})\\ &\subseteq(c-\delta,c+\delta)\cap(B\setminus\{c\}). \end{aligned} \]

As \(\delta>0\) was arbitrary, \(c\) is a cluster point of \(B\).

Taking \(A=S\cap(-\infty,c)\) and \(B=S\), we conclude that \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\).

Proof \((\Rightarrow)\). Suppose \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L\).

Let \(\epsilon>0\) and choose \(\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ 0<|x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \]

For \(x\in S\),

\[ \begin{aligned} c-\delta<x<c&\implies0<|x-c|<\delta\\ &\implies|f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Since \(\epsilon>0\) was arbitrary, \(\lim\limits_{x\to c^-}f(x)=L\).

For the right-hand limit, use the same \(\delta>0\).

For \(x\in S\),

\[ \begin{aligned} c<x<c+\delta&\implies0<|x-c|<\delta\\ &\implies|f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Again, \(\epsilon>0\) was arbitrary, so \(\lim\limits_{x\to c^+}f(x)=L\).

Proof \((\Leftarrow)\). Suppose both one-sided limits equal \(L\).

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Choose \(\delta_-,\delta_+>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ \begin{aligned} c-\delta_-<x<c&\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon,\\ c<x<c+\delta_+&\implies |f(x)-L|<\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Set \(\delta=\min\{\delta_-,\delta_+\}>0\).

Let \(x\in S\) with \(0<|x-c|<\delta\).

  • If \(x<c\), then \(c-\delta_-<x<c\).
  • If \(x>c\), then \(c<x<c+\delta_+\).

In both cases, \(|f(x)-L|<\epsilon\).

Hence \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=L\).

├── continuous-functions/
├── definition/

Definition. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(c\in S\).

We say \(f\) is continuous at \(c\) if

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists\delta>0\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\forall x\in S,\\ |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \end{gathered} \]

For \(U\subseteq S\), we say \(f\) is continuous on \(U\) if it is continuous at every point of \(U\).

Optional Remark. For \(A\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), we write

\[ C(A)=\{f:A\to\mathbb{R}:f\text{ is continuous on }A\}. \]

Thus \(C(A)\) is the set of continuous real-valued functions on \(A\).

Negation. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(c\in S\).

The function \(f\) is not continuous at \(c\) if and only if

\[ \begin{gathered} \exists\epsilon_0>0,\ \forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in S\quad\text{s.t.}\\ |x-c|<\delta\quad\text{and}\quad |f(x)-f(c)|\geq\epsilon_0. \end{gathered} \]
├── affine-functions-are-continuous/

Example. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\).

The function \(f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\), \(f(x)=ax+b\), is continuous on \(\mathbb{R}\).

Proof Sketch.

Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

WTS. \(\forall\epsilon>0,\ \exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

This is equivalent to

\[ |x-c|<\delta\implies |a|\,|x-c|<\epsilon. \]

This suggests taking \(\delta>0\) such that

\[ |a|\delta<\epsilon. \]

Proof. Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\epsilon>0\), and set \(\delta=\epsilon/(1+|a|)>0\).

If \(x\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(|x-c|<\delta\), then

\[ \begin{aligned} |f(x)-f(c)| &=|a|\,|x-c|\\ &\leq |a|\delta\\ &=\frac{|a|}{1+|a|}\epsilon<\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(f\) is continuous at every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

├── a-discontinuous-function/

Example. Define \(f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) by

\[ f(x)=\begin{cases} 1,&x\neq0,\\ 2,&x=0. \end{cases} \]

This function is not continuous at \(0\).

Recall. For \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\), \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), and \(c\in S\), \(f\) is not continuous at \(c\) if and only if

\[ \begin{gathered} \exists\epsilon_0>0,\ \forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in S\quad\text{s.t.}\\ |x-c|<\delta\quad\text{and}\quad |f(x)-f(c)|\geq\epsilon_0. \end{gathered} \]

Proof. For the example, choose \(\epsilon_0=1\) and let \(\delta>0\).

Set \(x=\delta/2>0\).

Then

\[ \begin{aligned} |x-0|&=\delta/2<\delta,\\ |f(x)-f(0)|&=|1-2|=1\geq\epsilon_0. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(f\) is not continuous at \(0\).

├── continuity-and-limits/

Theorem. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\in S\), and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

  1. If \(c\) is not a cluster point of \(S\), then \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).
  2. If \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\), then \[ f\text{ is continuous at }c \iff \lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c). \]

Recall. \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\) if \(\forall\delta>0\),

\[ (c-\delta,c+\delta)\cap(S\setminus\{c\})\neq\emptyset. \]

Thus \(c\) is not a cluster point of \(S\) if and only if \(\exists\delta_0>0\) s.t.

\[ (c-\delta_0,c+\delta_0)\cap(S\setminus\{c\})=\emptyset. \]

Since \(c\in S\), this is equivalent to

\[ (c-\delta_0,c+\delta_0)\cap S=\{c\}. \]

Proof (1). Suppose \(c\) is not a cluster point of \(S\).

Let \(\delta_0>0\) be such that

\[ (c-\delta_0,c+\delta_0)\cap S=\{c\}. \]

Let \(\epsilon>0\) and set \(\delta=\delta_0>0\).

For \(x\in S\),

\[ |x-c|<\delta\implies x=c. \]

Hence \(|f(x)-f(c)|=0<\epsilon\).

Thus \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).

Proof (2, \(\Rightarrow\)). Suppose \(c\) is a cluster point of \(S\) and \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

By continuity, \(\exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

In particular, for \(x\in S\),

\[ 0<|x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

Hence \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c)\).

Proof (2, \(\Leftarrow\)). Suppose \(\lim\limits_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c)\).

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Then \(\exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ 0<|x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

Let \(x\in S\) with \(|x-c|<\delta\).

  • If \(x\neq c\), then \(0<|x-c|<\delta\), so \(|f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon\).
  • If \(x=c\), then \(|f(x)-f(c)|=0<\epsilon\).

Thus \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).

├── sequential-characterization-of-continuity/

Theorem. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(c\in S\), and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

The following are equivalent:

  1. \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).
  2. For every sequence \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\) such that \(x_n\to c\), we have \(f(x_n)\to f(c)\).

Proof \((1\Rightarrow2)\).

Suppose \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).

We wish to show this implies the implication

\[ \text{if }\{x_n\}\subseteq S\text{ and }x_n\to c, \text{ then }f(x_n)\to f(c). \]

Let \(\epsilon>0\).

Let \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\) with \(x_n\to c\).

Observe

  • Continuity at \(c\) \(\implies\exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\), \[ |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]
  • \(x_n\to c\implies\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\) s.t. \[ \forall n\geq N,\quad |x_n-c|<\delta. \]

Fix \(\delta>0\) and \(N\in\mathbb{N}\) as above.

Altogether, if \(n\geq N\), then

\[ \begin{gathered} x_n\in S\quad\text{and}\quad |x_n-c|<\delta\\ (\text{by choice of }N) \end{gathered} \]

and so

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall n\geq N,\quad |f(x_n)-f(c)|<\epsilon\\ (\text{by choice of }\delta). \end{gathered} \]

Consequently, \(f(x_n)\to f(c)\).

Proof \((2\Rightarrow1)\).

Suppose: if \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\) and \(x_n\to c\), then \(f(x_n)\to f(c)\).

We wish to show that \(f\) is then continuous at \(c\).

Assume for contradiction \(f\) is not continuous at \(c\).

Then \(\exists\epsilon_0>0\) s.t. \(\forall\delta>0,\ \exists x\in S\) s.t.

\[ |x-c|<\delta \quad\text{and}\quad |f(x)-f(c)|\geq\epsilon_0. \]

Fix such an \(\epsilon_0\).

Since \(f\) is not continuous at \(c\),

\(\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ \exists x_n\in S\) s.t.

\[ |x_n-c|<\frac1n \quad\text{and}\quad |f(x_n)-f(c)|\geq\epsilon_0. \]

Fix such a sequence \(\{x_n\}\).

Altogether, \(\{x_n\}\subseteq S\) and

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad 0\leq|x_n-c|<1/n\\ (\text{by choice of }x_n). \end{gathered} \]

The squeeze theorem implies \(x_n\to c\), while

\[ \begin{gathered} \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad |f(x_n)-f(c)|\geq\epsilon_0>0\\ (\text{by choice of }\epsilon_0\text{ and }x_n). \end{gathered} \]

However, by assumption \(x_n\to c\) implies \(f(x_n)\to f(c)\), thereby establishing the contradiction

\[ 0=\lim_{n\to\infty}|f(x_n)-f(c)|\geq\epsilon_0>0. \]
├── sine-and-cosine-are-continuous/

Theorem. The functions \(\sin x\) and \(\cos x\) are continuous on \(\mathbb{R}\).

Recall. From the unit-circle definitions, for \(x\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ \sin^2x+\cos^2x=1, \] \[ |\sin x|\leq1,\qquad |\cos x|\leq1,\qquad |\sin x|\leq|x|. \]

For \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\), the angle-addition formula gives

\[ \sin(a+b)=\cos a\sin b+\sin a\cos b. \]

Proof (Sine). Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\epsilon>0\).

Observe

\[ \sin x-\sin c =2\sin\left(\frac{x-c}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{x+c}{2}\right). \]

Set \(\delta=\epsilon>0\).

If \(|x-c|<\delta\), then

\[ \begin{aligned} |\sin x-\sin c| &\leq2\left|\sin\left(\frac{x-c}{2}\right)\right|\\ &\leq2\frac{|x-c|}{2}=|x-c|<\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(\sin x\) is continuous at every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

Proof (Cosine). Recall \(\cos x=\sin(x+\pi/2)\).

Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and let \(\{x_n\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) with \(x_n\to c\).

Then

\[ x_n+\pi/2\to c+\pi/2. \]

Since sine is continuous, the sequential characterization gives

\[ \cos x_n=\sin(x_n+\pi/2)\to\sin(c+\pi/2)=\cos c. \]

By the sequential characterization, \(\cos x\) is continuous at every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

├── polynomials-are-continuous/

Theorem. Let \(d\in\mathbb{N}\) and \(a_0,\ldots,a_d\in\mathbb{R}\).

The polynomial \(f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) given by

\[ f(x)=a_dx^d+\cdots+a_1x+a_0 \]

is continuous on \(\mathbb{R}\).

Proof. Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\), and let \(\{x_n\}\) be a sequence in \(\mathbb{R}\) such that \(x_n\to c\).

The limit laws for sequences give

\[ \begin{aligned} \lim_{n\to\infty}f(x_n) &=\lim_{n\to\infty}(a_dx_n^d+\cdots+a_1x_n+a_0)\\ &=a_dc^d+\cdots+a_1c+a_0\\ &=f(c). \end{aligned} \]

By the sequential characterization for continuity, \(f\) is continuous at every \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

├── algebra-of-continuous-functions/

Theorem. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(f,g:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

If \(f\) and \(g\) are continuous at \(c\in S\), then

  1. \(f+g\) is continuous at \(c\).
  2. \(fg\) is continuous at \(c\).
  3. If \(g(x)\neq0\) for all \(x\in S\), then \(f/g\) is continuous at \(c\).

Proof. Use the sequential characterization of continuity and the algebra of sequence limits.

Optional Remark. Let \(A\subseteq\mathbb{R}\).

If \(f,g\in C(A)\) and \(\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\), then

\[ f+g,\quad \alpha f,\quad fg\in C(A). \]

These operations are defined pointwise: for \(x\in A\),

\[ \begin{aligned} (f+g)(x)&=f(x)+g(x),\\ (\alpha f)(x)&=\alpha f(x),\\ (fg)(x)&=f(x)g(x). \end{aligned} \]

They obey the usual algebraic rules, so we say that \(C(A)\) is an algebra over \(\mathbb{R}\).

Informally, we can add, scale, and multiply continuous functions and remain in \(C(A)\).

(Roughly speaking, an algebra is a vector space with "multiplication.")

├── composition-of-continuous-functions/

Theorem. Let \(A,B\subseteq\mathbb{R}\), let \(g:A\to B\) and \(f:B\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(c\in A\).

If \(g\) is continuous at \(c\) and \(f\) is continuous at \(g(c)\), then \(f\circ g:A\to\mathbb{R}\) is continuous at \(c\).

Proof. We appeal to sequential characterization of continuity.

Thus, if \(\{x_n\}\subseteq A\) and \(x_n\to c\), we wish to show

\[ (f\circ g)(x_n)=f(g(x_n))\to f(g(c))=(f\circ g)(c). \]

Observe that \(g\) continuous at \(c\) implies

\[ g(x_n)\to g(c). \]

Let \(y_n=g(x_n)\) and \(y=g(c)\), noting \(\{y_n\}\subseteq B\) and \(y_n\to y\).

\(f\) continuous at \(g(c)=y\) implies

\[ f(g(x_n))=f(y_n)\to f(y)=f(g(c)), \]

and so \(f\circ g\) is continuous at \(c\).

Optional Remark. The preceding theorem implies that the algebra \(C(\mathbb{R})\) is also closed under composition:

\[ f,g\in C(\mathbb{R})\implies f\circ g\in C(\mathbb{R}). \]

Composition gives \(C(\mathbb{R})\) an additional algebraic structure: it is associative, and the identity function \(x\mapsto x\) acts as an identity.

Unlike pointwise multiplication, composition need not be commutative.

├── continuity-examples/

Example. The function \(h:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\) given by

\[ h(x)=\frac1{x^2} \]

is continuous on \((0,\infty)\).

Proof. The polynomials \(x\mapsto1\) and \(x\mapsto x^2\) are continuous on \((0,\infty)\).

Since \(x^2>0\) for every \(x>0\), the quotient theorem gives continuity of \(h\).

Example. The function \(h:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\) given by

\[ h(x)=\left(\cos\frac1{x^2}\right)^2 \]

is continuous on \((0,\infty)\).

Proof. By the previous example and continuity of cosine, \(g:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\), \(g(x)=\cos(1/x^2)\), is continuous.

The polynomial \(p:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\), \(p(t)=t^2\), is continuous. Since \(h=p\circ g\), the composition theorem gives continuity of \(h\).

└── the-dirichlet-function/

Lemma (Density of the irrationals). Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a<b\).

There exists \(x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}\) such that

\[ a<x<b. \]

Proof. By density of the rationals, choose \(q\in\mathbb{Q}\) such that

\[ a-\sqrt{2}<q<b-\sqrt{2}. \]

Set \(x=q+\sqrt{2}\), noting \(x\) is irrational since otherwise \(x-q=\sqrt{2}\in\mathbb{Q}\).

Then \(a<x<b\), with \(x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}\).

Theorem. The Dirichlet function \(f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\), defined by

\[ f(x)=\begin{cases} 1,&x\in\mathbb{Q},\\ 0,&x\notin\mathbb{Q}, \end{cases} \]

is not continuous at any point of \(\mathbb{R}\).

Proof. First suppose \(c\in\mathbb{Q}\).

By density of the irrationals, choose a sequence \(\{x_n\}\) in \(\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}\) satisfying

\[ c<x_n<c+\frac1n\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}). \]

Then \(x_n\to c\), whereas

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}f(x_n)=0\neq1=f(c). \]

By the sequential characterization for continuity, \(f\) is not continuous at \(c\).

Now suppose \(c\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}\).

By density of the rationals, choose a sequence \(\{x_n\}\) in \(\mathbb{Q}\) satisfying

\[ c<x_n<c+\frac1n\qquad(n\in\mathbb{N}). \]

Then \(x_n\to c\), whereas

\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}f(x_n)=1\neq0=f(c). \]

The sequential characterization again rules out continuity at \(c\).

Thus \(f\) is not continuous at any point of \(\mathbb{R}\).

├── continuous-functions-on-closed-intervals/
├── bounded-functions/

Definition. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

We say \(f\) is bounded if \(\exists B\geq0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ |f(x)|\leq B. \]

Negation. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

The function \(f\) is unbounded (not bounded) if and only if

\[ \forall B\geq0,\ \exists x\in S\quad\text{s.t.}\quad |f(x)|>B. \]
├── boundedness-on-closed-intervals/

Theorem. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a\leq b\).

If \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) is continuous, then \(f\) is bounded.

Proof. Suppose for contradiction that \(f\) is unbounded.

Then \(\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\), \(\exists x_n\in[a,b]\) s.t.

\[ |f(x_n)|\geq n. \]

Select such a sequence \(\{x_n\}\).

By Bolzano–Weierstrass, there is a subsequence \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) and an \(x\in\mathbb{R}\) such that \(x_{n_k}\to x\).

Since \(a\leq x_{n_k}\leq b\), preservation of order gives

\[ a\leq x\leq b. \]

By continuity of \(f\) and \(x_{n_k}\to x\), there holds

\[ f(x_{n_k})\to f(x). \]

Thus \(\{|f(x_{n_k})|\}\) is convergent and hence bounded: \(\exists C>0\) s.t. \(\forall k\in\mathbb{N}\),

\[ |f(x_{n_k})|\leq C. \]

However, the subsequence indices satisfy \(n_k\geq k\), so

\[ \forall k\in\mathbb{N},\qquad k\leq n_k\leq|f(x_{n_k})|\leq C, \]

a contradiction.

Thus \(f\) is bounded.

├── absolute-minima-and-maxima/

Definition. Let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

We say \(f\) achieves an absolute minimum at \(c\in S\) if \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ f(c)\leq f(x). \]

We say \(f\) achieves an absolute maximum at \(d\in S\) if \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ f(x)\leq f(d). \]
\(\text{absolute max}\) \(\text{absolute min}\) \(f(d)\) \(f(c)\) \(d\) \(c\) \(-1\) \(1\) \(0\) \(y\) \(x\)
├── the-extreme-value-theorem/

Theorem (Extreme Value Theorem). Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a\leq b\), and let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) be continuous.

Then \(f\) achieves an absolute minimum and an absolute maximum.

Proof. We prove \(f\) has a maximum; the proof \(f\) has a minimum is similar.

The preceding theorem implies \(f\) is bounded.

If \(E=\{f(x):x\in[a,b]\}\), then \(E\) is nonempty and bounded.

Let \(L:=\sup E\).

We use Bolzano–Weierstrass to show \(L\) is the absolute maximum of \(f\).

In particular we aim to show \(\exists x\in[a,b]\) s.t. \(f(x)=L\).

Recall \(L=\sup E\), \(E=\{f(x):x\in[a,b]\}\).

Observe

  1. \(\forall x\in[a,b]\), \(f(x)\leq L\).
  2. \(\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\), \(\exists x_n\in[a,b]\) s.t. \[ L-\frac1n<f(x_n)\leq L. \]

    Note: this follows from the \(\epsilon\)-characterization of supremum, and from \(\forall y\in E\), \(\exists x\in[a,b]\) s.t. \(f(x)=y\).

Fix such a sequence \(\{x_n\}\subset[a,b]\), noting \(\lim f(x_n)=L\) by the squeeze theorem; a priori, \(\{x_n\}\) could diverge.

Bolzano–Weierstrass \(\implies\exists\) subsequence \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) and \(z\in\mathbb{R}\) s.t. \(x_{n_k}\to z\).

Observe

\[ \begin{aligned} a\leq x_{n_k}\leq b &\implies z\in[a,b],\\ f(x_n)\to L &\implies f(x_{n_k})\to L,\\ f\text{ continuous on }[a,b] &\implies f(z)=\lim_{k\to\infty}f(x_{n_k})=L. \end{aligned} \]

Consequently, since \(L=\sup\{f(x):x\in[a,b]\}\), we have shown

\[ \forall x\in[a,b],\quad f(x)\leq L=f(z). \]

Hence \(f\) achieves an absolute maximum at \(z\).

├── necessity-of-the-hypotheses/

Example. Continuity cannot be omitted from the Extreme Value Theorem.

Define \(f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}\) by

\[ f(x)=\begin{cases} \frac12,&x\in\{0,1\},\\ x,&0<x<1. \end{cases} \]

This function is not continuous at \(0\) or \(1\).

Its range is \((0,1)\), so it achieves neither an absolute minimum nor an absolute maximum.

No matter how close \(x\) is to \(0\) or \(1\), \(f(x)\) is never equal to \(0\) nor \(1\).

Example. The conclusion can fail on an open interval.

The function \(f:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}\) given by

\[ f(x)=\frac1x-\frac1{1-x} \]

is continuous.

For \(n\geq2\),

\[ \begin{aligned} f(1/n)&=n-\frac{n}{n-1}\geq n-2,\\ f(1-1/n)&=\frac{n}{n-1}-n\leq2-n. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(f\) is unbounded above and below, so it achieves neither an absolute maximum nor an absolute minimum.

} \P{3.30618}{3.9}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangle} \P{13.14382}{-4.15}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangledown} \phantom{\rule[-4.8em]{17em}{9.6em}} } \]
├── the-weak-intermediate-value-theorem/

Theorem. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a<b\), and let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) be continuous.

If \(f(a)<0<f(b)\), then \(\exists c\in(a,b)\) such that

\[ f(c)=0. \]

Proof. Roughly speaking, we construct monotone sequences \(\{a_n\},\{b_n\}\subset[a,b]\) satisfying

\[ \begin{gathered} a_n\to c,\qquad b_n\to c,\qquad c\in[a,b],\\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad f(a_n)<0\leq f(b_n). \end{gathered} \]

Continuity and preservation of order give \(f(c)\leq0\leq f(c)\), hence \(f(c)=0\).

Since \(f(a)<0<f(b)\), \(c\in(a,b)\).

We define the two sequences \(\{a_n\},\{b_n\}\subseteq[a,b]\) inductively:

  1. Define \(a_1=a\) and \(b_1=b\).
  2. Given \(a_n\) and \(b_n\), define \[ m_n=\frac{a_n+b_n}{2}. \]
  3. Define \(a_{n+1}\) and \(b_{n+1}\) by declaring \[ (a_{n+1},b_{n+1})= \begin{cases} (a_n,m_n),&f(m_n)\geq0,\\ (m_n,b_n),&f(m_n)<0. \end{cases} \]

Example. One possible construction.

Signs indicate the sign of \(f\); double segments are retained.

\[ { \def\BisectionPlace#1#2#3{\rlap{\kern#1em\raise#2em\hbox{$#3$}}} % Stage 1 \BisectionPlace{1}{11.93}{\rule{10em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{1}{12.07}{\rule{10em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{11}{12}{\rule{10em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{0.9825}{11.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{0.52}{13.5}{a_1} \BisectionPlace{0.61}{12.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{10.9825}{11.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{10.34}{13.5}{m_1=\tfrac12(a_1+b_1)} \BisectionPlace{10.61}{12.55}{+} \BisectionPlace{20.9825}{11.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{20.55}{13.5}{b_1} \BisectionPlace{20.61}{12.55}{+} % Stage 2 \BisectionPlace{6}{8.93}{\rule{5em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{6}{9.07}{\rule{5em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{1}{9}{\rule{5em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{0.9825}{8.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{0.52}{10.5}{a_2} \BisectionPlace{0.61}{9.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{5.9825}{8.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{5.34}{10.5}{m_2} \BisectionPlace{5.61}{9.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{10.9825}{8.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{10.55}{10.5}{b_2} \BisectionPlace{10.61}{9.55}{+} % Stage 3 \BisectionPlace{6}{5.93}{\rule{2.5em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{6}{6.07}{\rule{2.5em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{8.5}{6}{\rule{2.5em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{5.9825}{5.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{5.52}{7.5}{a_3} \BisectionPlace{5.61}{6.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{8.4825}{5.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{7.84}{7.5}{m_3} \BisectionPlace{8.11}{6.55}{+} \BisectionPlace{10.9825}{5.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{10.55}{7.5}{b_3} \BisectionPlace{10.61}{6.55}{+} % Stage 4 \BisectionPlace{7.25}{2.93}{\rule{1.25em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{7.25}{3.07}{\rule{1.25em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{6}{3}{\rule{1.25em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{5.9825}{2.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{5.52}{4.5}{a_4} \BisectionPlace{5.61}{3.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{7.2325}{2.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{6.59}{4.5}{m_4} \BisectionPlace{6.86}{3.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{8.4825}{2.84}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{8.05}{4.5}{b_4} \BisectionPlace{8.11}{3.55}{+} % Stage 5 \BisectionPlace{7.25}{0}{\rule{1.25em}{0.035em}} \BisectionPlace{7.2325}{-0.16}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{6.77}{1.5}{a_5} \BisectionPlace{6.86}{0.55}{-} \BisectionPlace{8.4825}{-0.16}{\rule{0.035em}{0.32em}} \BisectionPlace{8.05}{1.5}{b_5} \BisectionPlace{8.11}{0.55}{+} \phantom{\rule[-0.3em]{22em}{14.7em}} } \]

Heuristically: the sequences \(\{a_n\},\{b_n\}\) so constructed are monotonically converging to a common point.

Observe that \(\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\),

  1. The intervals \(\{(a_n,b_n)\}\) are nested: \[ a\leq a_n\leq a_{n+1}\leq b_{n+1}\leq b_n\leq b. \]

    Indeed,

    \[ a_n\leq\frac{a_n+b_n}{2}\leq b_n \]

    and at least one of \(a_{n+1}\) or \(b_{n+1}\) is \(\frac{a_n+b_n}{2}\) by construction.

  1. The lengths are halved: \[ b_{n+1}-a_{n+1}=\frac{b_n-a_n}{2}. \]

    Indeed,

    \[ (a_{n+1},b_{n+1})=(a_n,m_n)\quad\text{or}\quad(m_n,b_n) \]

    and

    \[ m_n-a_n=b_n-m_n=\frac{b_n-a_n}{2}. \]
  1. The endpoints \(a_n\) and \(b_n\) satisfy \[ f(a_n)<0\leq f(b_n). \]

    This can be proved by induction:

    The case \(n=1\) holds by hypothesis on \(a\) and \(b\).

    For the inductive step, suppose it holds for \(n\).

    Then

    • If \(f(m_n)\geq0\), then \((a_{n+1},b_{n+1})=(a_n,m_n)\), so \[ \begin{aligned} f(a_{n+1})&=f(a_n)<0,\\ f(b_{n+1})&=f(m_n)\geq0. \end{aligned} \]
    • If \(f(m_n)<0\), then \((a_{n+1},b_{n+1})=(m_n,b_n)\), so \[ \begin{aligned} f(a_{n+1})&=f(m_n)<0,\\ f(b_{n+1})&=f(b_n)\geq0. \end{aligned} \]

In short

\(\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\)

  1. \[ a\leq a_n\leq a_{n+1}\leq b_{n+1}\leq b_n\leq b. \]
  2. \[ b_{n+1}-a_{n+1}=\frac{b_n-a_n}{2}. \]
  3. \[ f(a_n)<0\leq f(b_n). \]

By 1., \(\{a_n\},\{b_n\}\) are monotonic and bounded.

Monotone Convergence Theorem \(\implies\exists c,d\in[a,b]\) s.t.

\[ a_n\to c,\qquad b_n\to d. \]

Iterating 2.:

\[ \begin{gathered} b_{n+1}-a_{n+1}=2^{-n}(b-a)\to0,\\ d-c=\lim(b_{n+1}-a_{n+1})=0, \end{gathered} \]

and so \(d=c\).

Consequently \(a_n,b_n\to c\in[a,b]\).

By continuity of \(f\), 3. and limits respecting order:

\[ f(c)=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(a_n)\leq0\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}f(b_n)=f(c) \]

and so \(f(c)=0\).

Lastly: since \(c\in[a,b]\) and \(f(a)<0<f(b)\), it follows that \(c\neq a,b\) and therefore \(c\in(a,b)\).

├── the-intermediate-value-theorem/

Theorem (Bolzano's Intermediate Value Theorem).

Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a<b\), and let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) be continuous.

If \(y\in\mathbb{R}\) satisfies

\[ f(a)<y<f(b)\quad\text{or}\quad f(b)<y<f(a), \]

then \(\exists c\in(a,b)\) such that \(f(c)=y\).

} \H2.05/2.75/.14;\H2.33/2.75/.14;\H2.61/2.75/.14;\H2.89/2.75/.14;\H3.17/2.75/.14;\H3.45/2.75/.14; \H3.73/2.75/.14;\H4.01/2.75/.14;\H4.29/2.75/.14;\H4.57/2.75/.14;\H4.85/2.75/.14;\H5.13/2.75/.14; \H5.41/2.75/.14;\H5.69/2.75/.14;\H5.97/2.75/.14;\H6.25/2.75/.14;\H6.53/2.75/.14;\H6.81/2.75/.14; \H7.09/2.75/.14;\H7.37/2.75/.14;\H7.65/2.75/.14;\H7.93/2.75/.14;\H8.21/2.75/.14;\H8.49/2.75/.14; \H8.77/2.75/.14;\H9.05/2.75/.14;\H9.33/2.75/.14;\H9.61/2.75/.14;\H9.89/2.75/.14;\H10.17/2.75/.14; \H10.45/2.75/.14;\H10.73/2.75/.14;\H11.01/2.75/.14;\H11.29/2.75/.14;\H11.57/2.75/.13;\V7.05/.14/.14; \V7.05/.42/.14;\V7.05/.7/.14;\V7.05/.98/.14;\V7.05/1.26/.14;\V7.05/1.54/.14;\V7.05/1.82/.14; \V7.05/2.1/.14;\V7.05/2.38/.14; \P{2.487}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{2.375}{1.625}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{2.3}{-.7}{\scriptstyle a} \P{7.037}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{6.925}{2.625}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{6.85}{-.7}{\scriptstyle c} \P{11.588}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{11.475}{3.625}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{11.4}{-.7}{\scriptstyle b} \P{1.85}{1.75}{\rule{.2em}{.025em}} \P{.45}{1.57}{\scriptstyle f(a)} \P{1.85}{2.75}{\rule{.2em}{.025em}} \P{1.15}{2.57}{\scriptstyle y} \P{1.85}{3.75}{\rule{.2em}{.025em}} \P{.45}{3.57}{\scriptstyle f(b)} \phantom{\rule[-1em]{13em}{5.5em}} } \]

Proof.

Suppose \(f(a)<y<f(b)\) and observe then \(f(a)-y<0<f(b)-y\).

This suggests introducing \(g(x)=f(x)-y\) and appealing to the preceding weak intermediate value theorem.

The case \(f(b)<y<f(a)\) is similarly handled.

Define \(g:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) by \(g(x)=f(x)-y\).

Then \(g\) is continuous and

\[ g(a)=f(a)-y<0<f(b)-y=g(b). \]

By the weak intermediate value theorem: \(\exists c\in(a,b)\) s.t.

\[ 0=g(c)=f(c)-y \]

and so \(f(c)=y\).

├── image-of-a-closed-interval/

Theorem. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a\leq b\), and let \(c,d\in[a,b]\).

Let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\) be continuous with minimum \(f(c)\) and maximum \(f(d)\).

Then

\[ f([a,b])=[f(c),f(d)]. \]
\[ { \rlap{\kern4em\hbox{$ \color{#3f6286} \def\C#1/#2;{\raise#1em\hbox{$\rule{.035em}{#2em}$}} \P{4}{-.06}{\rule{9.1em}{.12em}} \P{3.39}{1.845}{\rule{.12em}{2.309em}} \P{3.875}{-0.125}{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{12.975}{-0.125}{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{3.325}{1.72}{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{3.325}{4.03}{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{3.875}{2.875}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{5.911}{-0.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{5.798}{1.72}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{11.164}{-0.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{11.052}{4.03}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{12.975}{2.875}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{3.8}{-0.7}{\scriptstyle a} \P{12.9}{-0.7}{\scriptstyle b} \P{5.723}{-0.7}{\scriptstyle c} \P{10.977}{-0.7}{\scriptstyle d} \P{1.95}{1.665}{\scriptstyle f(c)} \P{1.95}{3.975}{\scriptstyle f(d)} \phantom{\rule[-1.6em]{14.5em}{7.2em}} } \]

Proof. Since

\[ \forall x\in[a,b],\quad f(c)\leq f(x)\leq f(d), \]

there holds

\[ f([a,b])\subseteq[f(c),f(d)]. \]

To achieve equality, we prove \([f(c),f(d)]\subseteq f([a,b])\); i.e.,

\[ \forall y\in[f(c),f(d)],\quad \exists z\in[a,b]\text{ s.t. }f(z)=y. \]

This will follow from IVT.

Let \(y\in[f(c),f(d)]\).

If \(y=f(c)\) or \(y=f(d)\), then \(y\in f([a,b])\) and we are done.

Suppose otherwise: \(f(c)<y<f(d)\), noting this implies \(c\neq d\).

Let \(u=\min\{c,d\},\ v=\max\{c,d\}\), noting \([u,v]\subseteq[a,b]\).

\P{2.524}{3.76}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{2.524}{4.04}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.035em}} \P{6.276}{.12}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{6.276}{.4}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{6.276}{.68}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{6.276}{.96}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{6.276}{1.24}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{6.276}{1.52}{\color{#929292}\rule{.022em}{.14em}} \P{2.524}{-.025}{\color{#3f6286}\rule{3.753em}{.075em}} \P{1.138}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{1.025}{2.875}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{2.511}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{2.399}{4.03}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{6.264}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{6.151}{1.72}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{7.638}{-.13}{\rule{.025em}{.26em}} \P{7.525}{2.875}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{4.275}{2.875}{\color{#3f6286}\scriptscriptstyle\bullet} \P{4.64}{3.35}{\scriptstyle y} \P{.95}{-.7}{\scriptstyle a} \P{7.45}{-.7}{\scriptstyle b} \P{1.674}{-.7}{\scriptstyle u=d} \P{5.426}{-.7}{\scriptstyle v=c} \P{2.764}{4.455}{\scriptstyle f(d)} \P{6.516}{1.295}{\scriptstyle f(c)} \P{3.7}{-1.35}{\scriptstyle [u,v]} \phantom{\rule[-2.8em]{8.9em}{8.8em}} } \]

Since \(\{u,v\}=\{c,d\}\), we have

\[ f(u)<y<f(v)\quad\text{or}\quad f(v)<y<f(u). \]

Also, \(u<v\) and \(f\) is continuous on \([u,v]\subseteq[a,b]\).

By IVT, \(\exists z\in(u,v)\subseteq[a,b]\) s.t. \(f(z)=y\).

Thus \(\exists z\in[a,b]\) s.t. \(f(z)=y\), which is what we needed to conclude

\[ [f(c),f(d)]\subseteq f([a,b]) \]

and whence

\[ f([a,b])=[f(c),f(d)]. \]
└── a-real-root-of-a-polynomial/

Theorem. The polynomial

\[ f(x)=x^5-x-1 \]

has at least one real root.

Proof. Since \(f\) is a polynomial, it is continuous on \([1,2]\).

Observe

\[ \begin{aligned} f(1)&=1-1-1=-1<0,\\ f(2)&=32-2-1=29>0. \end{aligned} \]

By the IVT, \(\exists c\in(1,2)\) such that \(f(c)=0\).

├── uniform-continuity/
├── recall-continuity/

Recall. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

Then \(f\) is continuous on \(S\) if \(\forall c\in S\) and \(\forall\epsilon>0\), \(\exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x\in S\),

\[ |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

When \(f\) is nonconstant and \(\epsilon>0\) is small, \(\delta\) will typically depend on \(\epsilon\).

In general, \(\delta\) may also depend on the point of continuity \(c\).

When \(\delta\) depends on both \(\epsilon\) and \(c\), we write \(\delta=\delta(\epsilon,c)\).

Example 1.

Consider \(f(x)=\sin x\), observing

\[ |f(x)-f(y)|\leq|x-y|,\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R}. \]

So, if any \(x\) and \(y\) are close, so too are the outputs \(f(x)\) and \(f(y).\)

In particular, \(\forall\epsilon>0\) and \(\forall c\in\mathbb{R}\), if \(\delta=\epsilon\), then

\[ \forall x\in\mathbb{R},\quad |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

In particular, \(\delta\) is independent of \(c\) and \(x\): all outputs \(f(x),f(c)\) are uniformly \(\epsilon\)-close if the inputs \(x,c\) are uniformly \(\delta\)-close.

Example 2. Let \(g(x)=\sin(1/x)\) on \((0,\infty)\). For \(k=1,3,5,\ldots\),

\[ \begin{aligned} \left|g\left(\frac{2}{k\pi}\right)-g\left(\frac{1}{k\pi}\right)\right|&=1,\\ \left|\frac{2}{k\pi}-\frac{1}{k\pi}\right|&=\frac{1}{k\pi}\longrightarrow0. \end{aligned} \]

Yet \(g\) is continuous on \((0,\infty)\).

Given a small \(\epsilon>0\), the smaller \(c>0\) is, the smaller \(\delta>0\) must be to get:

\[ \forall x>0,\quad |x-c|<\delta\implies |g(x)-g(c)|<\epsilon. \]

Thus \(\delta=\delta(\epsilon,c)\).
