├── defining-uniform-continuity/

Definition. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

We say \(f\) is uniformly continuous on \(S\) if \(\forall\epsilon>0\), \(\exists\delta>0\) s.t. \(\forall x,y\in S\),

\[ |x-y|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(y)|<\epsilon. \]

Remark. Here \(\delta=\delta(\epsilon)\) depends only on \(\epsilon\): the outputs \(f(x),f(y)\) are uniformly \(\epsilon\)-close whenever the inputs \(x,y\) are uniformly \(\delta\)-close.

├── uniform-continuity-of-the-square-function/

Example. The function \(f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}\) given by

\[ f(x)=x^2 \]

is uniformly continuous on \([0,1]\).

Proof. Let \(\epsilon>0\) and choose \(\delta=\epsilon/2>0\).

Let \(x,y\in[0,1]\) satisfy \(|x-y|<\delta\).

Since \(|x+y|\leq2\),

\[ \begin{aligned} |x^2-y^2|&=|x+y||x-y|\\ &\leq2|x-y|<2\delta=\epsilon. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(f\) is uniformly continuous on \([0,1]\).

├── negation-of-uniform-continuity/

Negation. Let \(S\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) and let \(f:S\to\mathbb{R}\).

Then \(f\) is not uniformly continuous on \(S\) if and only if \(\exists\epsilon_0>0\) s.t. \(\forall\delta>0\), \(\exists x,y\in S\) with

\[ |x-y|<\delta \quad\text{and}\quad |f(x)-f(y)|\geq\epsilon_0. \]

Remark. \(f\) not being uniformly continuous does not prevent \(f\) from being continuous.

└── uniform-continuity-on-closed-intervals/

Theorem. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) with \(a\leq b\), and let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}\).

Then \(f\) is continuous on \([a,b]\) if and only if \(f\) is uniformly continuous on \([a,b]\).

Proof (\(\Leftarrow\)). Suppose \(f\) is uniformly continuous.

Fix \(c\in[a,b]\) and \(\epsilon>0\).

Uniform continuity \(\implies\exists\delta>0\) s.t.

\[ \forall x,y\in[a,b],\qquad |x-y|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(y)|<\epsilon. \]

Taking \(y=c\), we obtain

\[ \forall x\in[a,b],\qquad |x-c|<\delta\implies |f(x)-f(c)|<\epsilon. \]

Thus \(f\) is continuous at \(c\).

Since \(c\in[a,b]\) was arbitrary, \(f\) is continuous on \([a,b]\).

Proof (\(\Rightarrow\)). Suppose \(f\) is continuous.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose \(f\) is not uniformly continuous.

Fix \(\epsilon_0>0\) from the negation.

Taking \(\delta=1/n\), for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) choose \(x_n,y_n\in[a,b]\) s.t.

\[ |x_n-y_n|<\frac1n \quad\text{and}\quad |f(x_n)-f(y_n)|\geq\epsilon_0. \]

Observe \(\{x_n\},\{y_n\}\) are bounded.

Bolzano–Weierstrass on \(\{x_n\}\) \(\implies\exists\) subsequence \(\{x_{n_k}\}\) and \(x\in[a,b]\) s.t. \(x_{n_k}\to x\).

Bolzano–Weierstrass on \(\{y_{n_k}\}\) \(\implies\exists\) subsequence \(\{y_{n_{k_j}}\}\) and \(y\in[a,b]\) s.t. \(y_{n_{k_j}}\to y\).

Note \(x_{n_{k_j}}\to x\).

Since \(n_{k_j}\geq k_j\geq j\), we have \(1/n_{k_j}\to0\).

Using \(|x_n-y_n|<1/n\) along the common subsequence,

\[ \begin{aligned} 0\leq |x-y| &=\lim_{j\to\infty}|x_{n_{k_j}}-y_{n_{k_j}}|\\ &\leq\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac1{n_{k_j}}=0. \end{aligned} \]

Thus \(x=y\), and both subsequences converge to \(y\in[a,b]\).

By continuity of \(f\) at \(y\) and the sequential characterization,

\[ f(x_{n_{k_j}})\to f(y),\qquad f(y_{n_{k_j}})\to f(y). \]

Hence

\[ \begin{aligned} 0=|f(y)-f(y)| &=\lim_{j\to\infty}|f(x_{n_{k_j}})-f(y_{n_{k_j}})|\\ &\geq\epsilon_0>0, \end{aligned} \]

a contradiction.

Therefore \(f\) is uniformly continuous on \([a,b]\).

└── derivatives/
├── definition-of-the-derivative/

Definition. Let \(I\subseteq\mathbb{R}\) be an interval containing more than one point, let \(f:I\to\mathbb{R}\), and let \(c\in I\).

We say \(f\) is differentiable at \(c\) if the limit

\[ \lim\limits_{x\to c}\frac{f(x)-f(c)}{x-c} \]

exists in \(\mathbb{R}\), with \(x\in I\setminus\{c\}\).

In this case, the derivative of \(f\) at \(c\) is

\[ f'(c):=\lim\limits_{x\to c}\frac{f(x)-f(c)}{x-c}. \]

Notation. If \(f\) is differentiable at every \(c\in I\), we say \(f\) is differentiable on \(I\).

Its derivative is the function

\[ f':I\to\mathbb{R},\qquad c\mapsto f'(c). \]

We also write \(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}x}\) for \(f'\).

├── derivatives-of-affine-functions/

Example. Let \(a,b\in\mathbb{R}\) and define \(f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) by

\[ f(x)=ax+b. \]

Then \(f\) is differentiable on \(\mathbb{R}\), and \(\forall c\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ f'(c)=a. \]

Proof. Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

For \(x\neq c\),

\[ \frac{f(x)-f(c)}{x-c} =\frac{ax+b-(ac+b)}{x-c} =a. \]

Hence

\[ f'(c)=\lim\limits_{x\to c}\frac{f(x)-f(c)}{x-c} =\lim\limits_{x\to c}a=a. \]
└── the-power-rule/

Example (The Power Rule). Let \(\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\), let \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), and define \(f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\) by

\[ f(x)=\alpha x^n. \]

Then \(f\) is differentiable on \(\mathbb{R}\), and \(\forall c\in\mathbb{R}\),

\[ f'(c)=\alpha n c^{n-1}. \]

For \(n=1\), this means \(f'(c)=\alpha\), including at \(c=0\).

Proof. The case \(n=1\) follows from the preceding example.

For \(n\geq2\), observe

\[ \begin{aligned} &(x-c)\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x^{n-1-j}c^j\\ &\quad=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x^{n-j}c^j -\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x^{n-1-j}c^{j+1}\\ &\quad=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x^{n-j}c^j -\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}x^{n-\ell}c^{\ell}\\ &\quad=x^n-c^n. \end{aligned} \]

The second equality uses \(\ell=j+1\); the common terms then cancel.

Let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\).

For \(x\neq c\), the identity gives

\[ \frac{\alpha x^n-\alpha c^n}{x-c} =\alpha\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}x^{n-1-j}c^j. \]

By the limit laws,

\[ \begin{aligned} f'(c) &=\lim\limits_{x\to c}\frac{\alpha x^n-\alpha c^n}{x-c}\\ &=\alpha\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}c^{n-1-j}c^j\\ &=\alpha n c^{n-1}. \end{aligned} \]
